Constitution of

The integrated multi modal transit network (IMMTN) envisages augmentation
of mass transport by providing adequate, accessible and affordable modes like
buses, mini buses, electric trolley buses, complimented by a network of rail
based mass rapid transit network like metro, commuter rail and light rail for
specific areas with due emphasis on intermediate public transport, non-
motorised transport and augmentation of infrastructure. Consequent upon
approval of the Cabinet in February 2006, to implement the IMMTN, a SPV,
the Delhi Integrated Multi-Modal Transit System (DIMTS), was established
as a public limited company under the Companies Act, with authorized capital
of हैं 1000 crore. The initial paid up capital of ¥ 100 crore was contributed by
the DOT under the scheme ‘Development of Alternative Modes of Transport’.
The Chief Secretary, Principal Secretary (Finance) and Commissioner
(Transport), GNCTD were the promoter Directors of the company and the
Board of Directors consisted of the Chief Secretary, GNCTD as Chairman and
Managing Director, four full time Directors and eight part time Directors. It
was also decided that financial gaps arising out of costs, financial and social
commitments mandated by the Government, shall be met with the financial
grants from the Government.

Subsequently, the Cabinet granted (July 2006), in-principle, approval to the
proposal for the joint venture (JV) between the DIMTS and the Infrastructure
Development and Finance Corporation (IDFC) with equal equity
participation. The Cabinet also authorized the DOT (June 2007) for signing of
the Shareholders’ Agreement with the IDFC which was signed in July 2007.
It was felt that inputs of a financial institution at the project preparation stage
would be very useful in promoting the role of private sector and minimizing
liability of the GNCTD.

It was seen that actual equity stake of the GNCTD in the JV stood at
49,99per cent, while IDFC was to have equal participation. Rest of the shares
of the DIMTS were held by minority shareholder since the inception of the
DIMTS, GNCTD has not, however, reflected its shares in the DIMTS, in its
Annual Finance Accounts. Detailed breakdown of the shareholding was
not made available to audit.

The guidelines on ‘Joint Ventures in Infrastructure Sector’ issued by the MoF
were applicable when a joint venture company was set up to formulate,
develop or implement any infrastructure projects or services associated with
it.

59


Audit Report No. 3 of the year 2013

The Cabinet approved (September 2008) allocation of various assignments on
nomination basis to the DIMTS and constitution of a committee under the
Chairpersonship of the Chief Secretary (CSC) with Principal Secretary
(Finance) and Commissioner (Transport) as members to determine the fee
structure for various works to be assigned to the DIMTS. Audit observed
thateven though the DIMTS was converted into a private company (July
2007), the DOT continued to favour it in allocation of works on nomination
basis. The DOT did not explore available opportunities to go for other
consultants through open bidding to have better terms in public favour. The
violation of laid down norms was, despite the repeated observations of the
Law Department of the GNCTD that the DIMTS-DOT agreements, including
the Integrated Mechanism Agreement (IMA) for Corporatisation of the
Private Stage Carriage Buses, were to minimize the responsibility of DIMTS
and extend maximum benefit to the company.

The DOT stated (October and December 2012) that it was evident from the
objectives, guiding the constitution of the company, restructuring its
management by converting it into 50:50 joint venture and binding provisions
of Shareholders’ agreement, projects assigned to the DIMTS as a matter of
government policy, were formally approved by the Cabinet through a series of
considered decisions on record. The Cabinet decisions abundantly fulfilled the
requirement of full justification for selection of a particular consultant under
special circumstances, as stipulated in GFRs’ and the requirement of detailed
justification for outsourcing a specific organization as stipulated in GFRs*.
The DOT further stated (March 2013) that the DIMTS had been created as a
single window to provide the entire gamut of services needed to integrate
different elements of the city-wide transportation system.

The reply, however, failed to address the audit observation that even after the
DIMTS became a non-Government Company, the DOT continued to allot
works to it on nomination basis. Rule 176 of GFRs relates only to
‘consultancy by nomination’ whereas the DOT, following a piecemeal
approach, allotted implementation of various projects such as, Automatic
Vehicle Tracking and Location System (AVLS) related services,
implementation of automatic fare collection system, managing fare collection
personnel and related services, and Integrated Mechanism for Corporatization
of Private Stage Carriage Buses (Cluster Scheme) to DIMTS, on nomination
basis.

The reply is not acceptable as the DIMTS is a private company. As per the
instructions on JVs in Infrastructure Sectors, if the public sector entity owns
more than 50 per cent shares, the JV would be regarded as a public sector
entity. However, if the share of public sector entity is 50 per cent or less, then
the JV is a private sector company and would, therefore, not be accountable to
the Government, Public Accounts Committee, Committee on Government
Undertakings, C&AG, etc.Nor would the Government rules relating to
procurement and expenditure apply to such a JV. Such a JV must, therefore,

*Rule 176 of GFRs (Annexure-4.4).
“Rule 184 of GFRs (Annexure-4.4)

60


Chapter-4 : CCO Based Audit

be treated at par with other private companies and any procurement of goods
or services from such a JV must follow the normal tendering processes as per
GFR.

The DOT further stated (March 2013) that the services obtained from DIMTS
were in the nature of management services, software solutions and integration
etc. and not as suppliers of materials and that DIMTS was a management
consultant and not an operator.

The reply is not acceptable as DIMTS was engaged not only as consultant but
also as implementer of projects as brought out in paragraph 4.1.5.2
(vii) here. Further, as regards the contention that DIMTS was a consultant
providing various services, the GOI Manual of Policies and Procedures of
Employment of Consultants (August 2006) provides for detailed process of
engaging a consultant through a transparent bid process unless the
consultancy was for %10 lakh or less when consultants could be hired on
nomination basis. However, audit scrutiny revealed that DIMTS has already
been paid in excess %10 lakh as discussed in paragraphs 4.1.5.2 (vi), (vii)
and 4.1.5.5 (i). Further, the engagement of DIMTS at project formulation
stage and at implementation stage was in contravention of the CVC order’.

The DIMTS did the initial work related to formulating the detailed scheme,
preparation of bid document and bid evaluation for ‘Corporatization of Private
Stage Carriage Buses in Delhi,’ at a fee of € 45 lakh. Audit observed that
DOT released (July 2008) an additional payment of € one crore, as asked by
DIMTS, for preparation of report on project structure, revenue model and
business plan without verifying or monitoring the work done by it, which was
unjustified.

The DOT stated (November 2012 and March 2013) that amount was not
additional as it was incurred on account of additional work which included
obtaining inputs based on best international practices with respect to project
structuring, revenue model, legal advice, business plan of PPP projects for the
cluster scheme in consultation with international experts.

The reply is not acceptable as the scope of work approved (October 2007) by
the DOT at a fee of हैं 45 lakh included works of detailed formulation of
scheme, submission of concept document, draft tender document, final bid
evaluation report, placement of orders on operators, signing of agreement and
formulation of revenue sharing model covering the complete life cycle of the
consultancy for scheme that would encompass project structuring and
formulation of revenue sharing model as well.

Despite the fact that the DIMTS was privatised in July 2007 and other
government agencies like the PWD, the DSIIDC, the DTC etc. were available
options for civil works and Project Management Consultant, the DOT

The DOT stated (March 2013) that the DIMTS was the only specialized

with end to end delivery. The DOT further replied that the DIMTS was not

the DOT

The DOT recognized the conflict between two roles of the DIMTS - ‘a major
player in the sector’ and ‘advisor to the department’ and was of the view that
DIMTS can perform the dual role, provided DOT was equipped with a
technical cell, which could be created from outsourced personnel, to review
and assess the functioning of DIMTS. The personnel in the cell would be
transport engineers, planners, economists, project appraisal experts and allied
research staff.

Para 11.3 of the Cabinet decision (No. 1473 dated 30 September 2008), of the
Cabinet stated that in order to obyiate any question of bias, it was necessary
that the independent technical cell be made available to the DOT to scrutinize
and give neutral advise on the proposal of the DIMTS, where they were
interested parties.

Audit scrutiny revealed that after establishing DIMTS in April 2006, the DOT
dropped its technical cell, even though after the Shareholders'agreement with
IDFC was signed in July 2007, DIMTS became a private company.

62


Chapter-4 : CCO Based Audit

Thus, in absence of independent technical cell, an assurance about the
unbiased technical scrutiny of DIMTS proposals received by DOT and other
agencies could not be drawn. In fact, the DOT continued to clear proposed
payments without appropriate technical vetting. Non-concurrence of the
Finance Department further compounded the irregularities in clearing these
proposals and making payments.

The DOT stated (March 2013) that DIMTS was not awarded the execution of
works and on the contrary it was awarded the task of monitoring the execution
of works on behalf of the DoT.

The reply was not tenable as the observation was related to DOT’s capacity to
examine proposals from consultants/agencies. Reply of the DOT confirmed
that the requisite expertise was not available with the DOT and it did not
independently scrutinize the designs and proposals submitted by DIMTS.

DIMTS was incorporated in April 2006 as a public limited company, and
subsequently,a JV with IDFC was created. It was also decided that the Chief
Secretary (CS) would be the ex-officio Chairman of the JV. CS along with
other senior bureaucrats, who hold various positions in the Board of Directors
of the DIMTS, decide the fee payable to the DIMTS for various works. Thus,
the presence of CS as Chairman of the Board, presented a situation of
potential conflict of interest, as the DIMTS was a private company.

The DOT stated (December 2012 and March 2013) that CS and other
members of CS committee being also on the Board of Directors of the
DIMTS, did not in any way mean that they would not take decisions in best
public interest. In fact, all these officials are senior bureaucrats and they are in
a fiduciary capacity of a nominee director to take care of the shareholder
interests (viz. government).

The Department of Expenditure, Ministry of Finance, Gol, in guidelines (July
2009) for joint venture in Infrastructure Sectors, remarked about conflict of
interest that, it was, normally, not advisable for government officials to
become chairpersons or hold other offices in a JV where the shareholding of
private sector entities is 50 per cent or more as it could give a misconception
of the JV being a government company or the JV availing of unintended
benefits or favour. The DOT could not offer any justification for this
arrangement. Further, no independent Director had been appointed in the
BoDs of the DIMTS as per the Shareholders’ agreement.

Secretary for finalizing IM fee

As per Cabinet decision No. 1466 dated 22.09.2008, it was decided that the
DIMTS shall be paid three per cent of the gross revenue from the operators
including fare box and advertising revenue to meet the cost of providing
service as IM. Within a week’s time, a Committee (the CS Committee) headed
by Chief Secretary was constituted under another Cabinet decision No. 1473
dated 30.09.2008, to decide the terms of payment to the DIMTS for taking up
assignments. Audit observed that until May 2012, the CS committee allowed

63


Audit Report No. 3 of the year 2013

the DOT to continue with the existing arrangement of payment of a fixed
amount of = 50 lakh per month, in violation of earlier cabinet decision for
payment of fee at three per cent of fare box collection. Thus, the decision of
finalization of fee paid to DIMTS, taken by the Committee was not in order.
The DOT stated (March 2013) that it was a transitional arrangement and three
per cent of the DOT collectibles was with reference to the entire cluster
scheme. Further, IM had to incur both fixed and variable cost components and
the fixed costs would have to be incurred till the critical mass of 1000 buses
was achieved.

The reply confirmed the facts pointed out by audit which indicated violation
of the Cabinet decision on fee for the IM.

4.1.5 Review of projects management and implementation of

selected schemes.

The review of operational performances of the selected schemes was carried
out to evaluate whether the planning and implementation of schemes was
efficient, effective and economical, and internal control and monitoring
mechanism for schemes was in place and effective.

4.1.5.1 Development of alternate mode of transport

The objective of the scheme was to create sustainable multi-modal transport,
such as High Capacity Bus System, Monorail and Light Rail Transit to
supplement the Metro Rail, DTC and other bus services.

The GNCTD planned a city wide public transport network to augment
transport services in Delhi. Under this plan, 38 corridors were proposed to be
implemented under different modes of transport. These corridors were
proposed to be implemented in three phases - Phase-I by 2011, Phase-II by
2016 and Phase-III by 2021. In the Phase - I, 14 Bus Rapid Transit (BRT)
corridors for introduction of public transit systems were identified and
approved by the GNCTD.

On the directions/orders of Delhi High Court on traffic issues in Delhi, the
GNCTD constituted a special task force under the chairmanship of Chief
Secretary, which decided (January 2011) that 14 corridors would be taken up
for preparation of Detailed Project Reports (DPRs), seven each by DIMTS
and Public Works Department (PWD). Accordingly, the DOT issued (April
2011) work order to DIMTS, on nomination basis, for preparation of DPRs of
seven corridors covering 115.6Kms at a total cost of हैं 36.99 crore (% 32
lJakh/km). On the request of the DIMTS, the DOT released first installment of
रू 9.25 crore to DIMTS (May 2011) for the work.

(6). Irregular expenditure of रैं 9.25 crore for preparation of DPRs of BRT
corridors

The DOT released first installment of हैं 9.25 crore (May 2011) to DIMTS
without appropriate approval of the Finance Department, which was irregular.
The Finance Department instructed (July 2011) DOT to ask for refund of the
amount from DIMTS.

The DOT stated (December 2012) that assignment of work to DIMTS was as
per the Cabinet decision of March 2011. The reply was not acceptable since it

Chapter-4 : CCO Based Audit

was decided in the 11"meeting of the Special Task Force in January 2011,
convened by the Chief Secretary that “the DIMTS was already doing work for
the DOT, they should also be shortlisted for taking up works for BRT

(March 2011). After being pointed out by Audit (August 2012), the DOT sent

(ti) Award of work to DIMTS for preparation of DPRs at unjustified

Scrutiny of records revealed that the cost of preparation of DPRs, as quoted by

The DOT stated (March 2013) that there was huge difference between scope
of work of DPR requisitioned from DIMTS by the DOT and the DPR
prepared by the PWD on account of the fact that scope of work of DPR
awarded to PWD did not have ITS component, Operational Plan International
input and peerreview in its scope. Further, PWD would add agency charges
and PWD’s consultants would end the engagement with submission of the
Report and would not provide support for clearances from EPCA, UTTIPEC.

The reply could not be verified by Audit as the letter for award of work
(outlining scope) to PWD as well as DIMTS was not shared with Audit.
Further, the Chief Minister had directed that work of all the corridors be
undertaken by PWD and the Finance Department had observed that fund
released to DIMTS was very high.

Corpora

The objective of the scheme was to gradually phase out the existing private
stage carriage buses (blue-line) in such a manner as to synchronize the
phasing out with the induction of the fleet of new buses with private
participation through companies including those formed by the existing or
prospective operators.

In July 2007, the DOT decided to revamp the bus system to provide a reliable,
safe and comfortable travel to the citizens and launched a scheme to
corporatize the private Stage Carriage Buses in Delhi. Accordingly, it
entrusted (August 2007) the task of providing consultancy for formulating the
detailed scheme, preparation of bid document and bid evaluation to the
DIMTS, at a fee of हैं 45 lakh. The DIMTS submitted (October 2007) a
‘Report on methodology for operations of privately owned stage carriage
buses in Delhi’. The Council of Ministers (CoM) vide the Cabinet decision of
November 2007, decided that:

> — The buses may be low-floor, semi low-floor and standard floor type,

> The principles and terms and conditions of the revenue sharing model
should be formulated and finalized in the bid document, and

>>. It would be pre-mature to consider the issue of subsidy.

65


Audit Report No. 3 of the year 2013

DIMTS further forwarded the ‘Request for Qualification’ (RFQ) in January
2008, Request for Proposal’ (RFP) in July 2008, Report on Business Model in
August 2008 to DOT and recommended the ‘Gross Cost’ based selection
system with introduction of incentives and Unified Time Table (UTT).In the
‘Gross Cost System’, the operators are contracted to provide service and are
compensated on the basis of bid amount towards providing that service. In
such system, the passenger revenue are retained by the concessioning
authority. In the ‘Net Cost System’ passenger fares go to service provider and
the bid amount is the estimated difference between the cost of providing the
service and collection. All stakeholders - DOT, Planning Department, Finance
Department and DIMTS decided collectively to explore the Gross Cost model
in Cluster 1. The Cabinet approved (September 2008) modified gross cost
system which provided for built in incentives and disincentives to ensure good
performance.

Accordingly, DOT issued RFP (December 2008) along with draft Concession
Agreement to seven pre-qualified applicants. Clause1.30.3 of RFP document
mentioned that the proposal of the bidders would be evaluated based on
payment by the DOT to bidder on ‘Consolidated Year One Fare’ (CYOF). In
response to RFP, only four bidders submitted their bids which were evaluated
by the ‘Evaluation Committee’. Consequently, M/s Star Bus Service Pvt. Ltd.
(L-1) emerged as successful bidder quoting lowest CYOF of % 85.78 crore
including capital cost (inclusive of depreciation and finance cost),
consumables, manpower, overhead charges and short term marginal rate.

For managing the operation of private stage carriage buses, DOT entered.
(April 2011) into an ‘Integrated Mechanism (IM) Agreement’ with DIMTS on.
nomination basis. The amended Article 5 of the IM Agreement prescribed the
procedure for receipts and disbursements in respect of the scheme.

Though, 17 clusters consisting of 657 routes were planned, DOT was not able
to make cluster one to five fully operational as it failed to provide depot space.
As of March 2012, the average number of buses on road was 208 against the
planned induction of 351 buses for four clusters (one, three, four and five) and
against service of 912 buses as per the concessionaire agreements for
clustersone to five.

(i) Adoption of business model

The DOT recorded (April 2008) on its file that disadvantages of the gross cost
model outweighed the advantages. A case study of Indore City Transport
Services Ltd. revealed that net cost model was running quite successfully. It
was further recorded that if the gross cost model was adopted, there might be
cash outgo from the Government, of about % 21 crore in first year in the first
cluster which would be %350 crore for 17 clusters on a proportional basis. if
assumptions were correct. The Planning and Finance departments had also
stressed on a self financing and self sustaining model without involving any
subsidy to the private operators, and the DOT also did not find the gross cost
model viable. It was only after the discussion held under the chairpersonship
of Chief Secretary, that the gross cost model was chosen to be explored for
Cluster 1, and the same was presented to the Cabinet as favourable model for
Delhi. When in principle approval of the draft concession agreement was
sought (September 2008), it was mentioned that the technical, financial and

66


Chapter-4 : CCO Based Audit

legal aspects would again be got vetted from Finance/Law Department before
releasing these documents to prequalified applicants. At the same time, it was
also decided that the financial support to be provided by the State would be
decided based on the evaluation of the bids once they were received.

Jt was also decided that the operational results of the Cluster 1 would be
evaluated before embarking on subsequent clusters. Thus, the DOT committed
to the gross cost model involving fund outgo from government. Scrutiny of
records further revealed that Cabinet approved (November 2009) the issue of
RFQ and RFP for remaining 16 clusters on this model without analyzing the
operational results of the Cluster 1. The DOT accordingly, floated the bid
documents for the Clusters 2 to 9 on the gross cost model. During finalization
of the bidding for the Cluster 6 to 9, the Finance Department again remarked
that the proposed cluster scheme, to be introduced in all 17 clusters, would
result in annual financial implication of र 778 crore with the provision of
upward increase in Consumer Price Index, cost of CNG etc, which appeared
to be more than the Government support to DTC to meet its operational losses
and the liability to be borne by the Government for next 10 years Finance
Department felt that a self-financing model may be devised and adopted so
that there was no burden on the public exchequer on this account. Despite the
repeated objections of the Finance Department, the DOT did not take up the
matter with DIMTS to devise a different model whereby, the outflow from the
public exchequer could be controlled. Thus, DOT headed further with the
same model for other clusters, without waiting for the result of performance of
Cluster], which involved substantial fund outgo from the Government to
private players.

Audit observed that the DOT released ₹ 42.89 crore to private operators for
the operation of four clusters (1, 3, 4 and 5) against the revenue collection of
%27.41 crore during 2011-12.

The DOT stated (December 2012) that gross cost model was approved by the
Council of Ministers and the subsequent introduction of buses under the
cluster scheme was entirely bonafide and had bome rigorous scrutiny of the
High Court of Delhi. It further stated (March 2013) that the cluster 1 could
not be operationalised, primarily due to non availability of depot space. It also
stated that the Government continued to support the DTC heavily through
deficit financing and other means.

The reply was not acceptable and relevant as it did not address the issue of the
scheme being self sustaining. Further, floating of bid documents for clusters 2
to 9 without the evaluation of Cluster] results, compelled the DOT to
continue with a potentially unsustainable option involving financial outgo
from the Government. The contention of the DOT regarding the urgency of
phasing out blue line buses for compliance with the orders of the High Court
without waiting for the review of performance of cluster 1 was also not
acceptable as non-provisioning of depot space, in terms of concession
agreement, indicated that the DOT did not take into account all factors and
limitations before implementing the project. This was corroborated by the fact
that the DOT could not augment the city bus fleet up to 11000 (private buses
and DTC buses in each cluster of routes was proposed to be 50:50) by the end
of March 2009 as per the directions of the High Court. It even failed to

67


Audit Report No. 3 of the year 2013

comply with the Cabinet direction for completing bids against all clusters by
June 2009. Further, comparison with DTC was not relevant as the
Corporatisation of Stage Carriage Buses was to eliminate the Blue Line Buses
which were run on self sustaining basis. Further, during approval (July
2012)of award of concessions for Cluster 6 to 9, the DOT itself had held that
the Business Model was not acceptable to it. Adoption of the business model
entailed a fund outgo to the tune of 715.48 crore during 2011-12,though only a
partial rollout of the scheme was done.

(ii) Incorrect inclusion of DIMTS’ fee in gap funding

Rule 176 of GFRs and CVC order of July 2007 inter-alia state that tendering
process is a basic requirement for the award of contract by any Government
agency. Moreover, the Viable Gap Funding (VGF) guidelines for PPP project
by the Gol explicitly statethat a private company shall be eligible for VGF
only if it is selected on the basis of open competitive bidding. Audit observed
that DIMTS had been paid % 7.19 crore as IM fee though it was selected on
nomination basis. DOT also entered into agreements with DIMTS for
implementation of ‘automatic fare collection system using smart card enabled
electronic ticketing machines’ (June 2011) and for ‘management of fare
collection personnel and related services in cluster buses’ (May 2011) for
which DIMTS was paid ₹ 35.32 lakh and %5.53 crore respectively during the
period May 2011 to June 2012. Since DIMTS was nominated for the above
mentioned works, total fee of = 13.07 crore paid to it, should not have been
considered while computing the viable gap funding requirements.

The DOT in its reply stated (December 2012 and March 2013) that DIMTS
was paid its fee which did not constitute viable gap funding. The viability gap
funding is made to bus concessionaire in the shape of amount of gap between
the carnings from the sale of tickets and CYF amount payable.

The reply is factually incorrect as while replenishing the Designated Account,
DOT not only paid the difference between actual collections and CYF payable
to concessionaires but also the related expenses as DIMTS fee for working as
IM, fee for implementing automatic fare collection system and fee for
management of fare collection personnel and related services in the form of
payments adjusted with collections.

(iii) Non-involvement of independent monitor in bid evaluation process

A scrutiny of the bids evaluation process revealed that there were two persons
nominated by the DOT to oversee how DIMTS carried the open tendering
process and selected the successful bidder. Despite the fact ‘the
Corporatization of the Private Stage Carriage Buses’ was a PPP project, no
independent monitor, as a representative from the Finance Department or Law
Department etc. was involved in the process to ensure the transparency in the
tendering process.

The DOT stated (December 2012 and March 2013) that it was wrong to
conclude that the cluster bidding process had no independent monitoring as
the evaluation of the bid and recommendation were duly got vetted from the
Finance Department as well as Law Department.

The reply of the Department was not acceptable because the role of an
independent monitor, having an independent position in relation to sponsoring

68


Chapter-4 : CCO Based Audit

authority was to monitor and record the conduct of participants and
proceedings particularly during the tendering and contract finalization stages.
However, fact remains that no independent monitor was present during the bid
evaluation.

(iv) Designated Account

After the draft ‘Integrated Mechanism Agreement’ (IMA) was vetted
(December 2010) by the Law Department, the DOT did not send the
agreement for comments of the Finance Department, even though it involved
financial liability in the form of IM fee, as well as establishment of
‘Designated Account’ (DA) by DIMTS in the name of ‘Bus Concessions
Integrated Management Fund’. As per the agreement, all the receipts
(collected through fare collection mechanism and any other sources such as
advertising revenue etc) were to be deposited into DA. The DOT was initially
required to put in the DA, six months advance of estimated payments to be
made to concessionaire.

(a) Release of 30.00 crore for DA without due approval of Legislature

DOT released (May 2011) %20 crore as advance to DIMTS for depositing in
DA without prior approval of the Finance Department and against financial
rules, by debiting non-plan head-3055-D.3 (3) (1) —‘Subsidy to DTC’ for
concessional passes. The DOT sent a proposal to the Finance Department to
change the nomenclature of head 3055-D. 3 (3) (2)-‘Grant-in-Aid to DTC
(Non-plan)’ to 3055-D. (3) (3) (2) - ‘Operation of Cluster Buses under
Corporatization scheme, IM, AVLS and AFCS (Non plan)’ and requested
subsequently to transfer 20 crore from head 3055-D. 3 (3) (2)-Compensation
for meeting deficit of cluster buses to head 3055-D. 3 (3) (1)-Subsidy to DTC
for concessional passes (Non-plan) to regularize the expenditure made out of
this head. Even though Finance Department asked DOT to get back the
released amount from DIMTS, it had to agree to the proposals, as fait
accompli, as DIMTS had already spent the amount and recovery was not
possible. Finance Department again sanctioned २ 10 crore (March 2012) and
र₹ 12.50 crore (June 2012) for replenishment of DA as interim measure subject
to overall review of the cluster scheme.

The procedure for creation and management of DA along with change in
nomenclature of the budget head was in violation of GFRs*, Delegation of
Financial Powers Rules’ and Transaction of Business Rules* of GNCTD. It
was also seen that the Detailed Demand for Grant for 2012-13 depicted the
head-3055-D (3) (3)(2) as ‘Compensation for meeting deficit of cluster buses’
in English and as ‘Grant in aid to DTC’ in its Hindi description. Thus, the
creation of DA and change of nomenclature leading to introduction of
concerned head in the budget was incorrect for want of necessary
administrative and legislative approvals.

The Department stated (October 2012)that vide minutes of meeting held in
February 2012 the concurrence of Finance Department (Budget) to transfer
6 Rule 57 of GFRs (Annexure-4.4)

7 Rule 10 (4) of Delegation of Financial Powers Rules (Annexure=4.4)
*Rule 5 (6) (d) of Transaction of Business rule, 1993 of GNCTD (Annexure:4.4)

Audit Report No. 3 of the year 2013

% 20 crore for meeting deficit of cluster buses was accorded by Principal
Secretary (Finance).

The reply is not acceptable as the Finance Department accorded its approval
in February 2012 as fait accompli and also at the same time maintained that
replenishment of DA was interim measure subject to overall review of the
scheme of corporatization of bus cluster. The DOT stated (March 2013) that
the amount was released by apportioning the amount to a new sub-head under
the existing major head of “3055-Road Transport” which was an existing
scheme under the budget approved by the legislature.

The reply of the DOT failed to address the audit contention regarding
procedure for creation and management of DA along with change in
nomenclature of the budget head which was in violation of GFRs, Delegation
of Financial Powers Rules and Transaction of Business Rules of GNCTD.

(b) Parking of government revenue in Designated Account

As the DOT adopted the gross cost model, revenue collected was required to
be deposited in government account as per codal provisions. While agreement
provides for a fare collection service provider to collect the fare and deposit it
in DA, Audit observed that the DIMTS, who was appointed as fare collection
service provider on nomination basis, utilized the collected amount from DA,
towards its own fees and CYF to the concessionaires and the DOT replenished
DA to bridge the gap between total revenue and total expenditure.

Hence, parking of government revenue in DA was against the spirit of ‘gross
cost system’ and also without the concurrence of the Finance Department.
Since, there is no provision for keeping government receipts outside the
government account, the procedure of operation of DA through DIMTS was
irregular.

The DOT stated (March 2013) that amounts collected as fares were intended
to meet the expenses towards operation of cluster scheme and thus, the
conclusion that depositing these sums into DA is against the Gross Cost
Model, is incorrect.

The reply was not acceptable as DOT itself earlier recorded that in Gross Cost
Model system, the passenger revenue is retained by concessioning authority,
thereby, transferring the revenue risk to concessioning authority. Therefore,
the DOT should have ensured that all revenues are being collected and
handed over properly.

(c) Monitoring of deposit of passenger fare receipts in DA

As per the Clause 5.1.1 of IMA, the DIMTS maintained the DA at Syndicate
Bank, Delhi Secretariat Branch. Scrutiny of the records revealed that apart
from this account, DIMTS had also opened other bank accounts in which
passenger fare collections were first deposited and transferred to DA later.
Though, the DIMTS played dual role of collection of fare revenue as well as
maintenance of related accounts, the DOT did not have any mechanism to
monitor timely and correct deposit of receipts in DA. Article 4.1.26 of the ‘IM
Agreement’ permits the DOT to inspect the accounts and records in respect of
services rendered by the DIMTS. However, no such inspection of accounts
and records was conducted by the DOT.

70


Chapter-4 : CCO Based Audit

The DOT stated (December 2012 and March 2013) that the mechanism of
reconciliation at department level was based on monthly statement of revenue
collected and deposited in the DA from the other accounts in the form of
monthly utilization certificates and depot-wise/cluster-wise/route-wise
monthly revenue statement.

The reply failed to address the issue of non-monitoring of receipts deposited
into DA, given the fact that the DIMTS played a dual role. Moreover, DOT
did not conduct any inspection or audit of accounts or records of revenue and
expenditure rendered by the DIMTS.

(v) Extended period of two years of IMA over concession agreement
period resulting in additional liability of = 12.00 crore

Scrutiny of records revealed that the period of concession agreement was 10
years whereas it was 12 years for IM agreement, which was objected to by the
Law Department on the ground that the DIMTS was not a concessionaire that
has to recoup its upfront investment in capital assets over a long duration.
Thus, the extended period of two years of IMA over concession agreement
period resulted in additional contractual liability of = 12.00 crore on the DOT.

The DOT stated (October 2012) that the IMA with the DIMTS did not have a
term of 12 years as it has been appointed for the period till the operation of the
bus clusters and further reasonable period thereafter, required for winding up
the operations of the bus clusters and transition to any new scheme. The DOT
further stated (March 2013) that the DOT reserved the right at its option to
extend the concession agreement by a further period of up to two years by
notifying the concessionaire at least one month prior to the expiry of the
period.

The reply was not convincing as Article 1 of the IMA defines ‘expiry’ as
‘expiry of agreement by efflux of time at the end of 12 years from the
appointed date’ meaning ‘date of the agreement’. Further, Article 2.1
anticipates transition to new scheme implying DIMTS would continue on
nomination basis. The reply was also not acceptable as the extension is
contingent on the DOT taking a decision at the end of the concession period,
whereas the term for the IM was for extended period of two years from the
beginning itself. Further, the Law Department had also suggested that the term
of the DIMTS appointment should be kept only for one year, extendable
further (at the sole discretion of the DOT) from time to time.

(vi) Unfavourable clause in IMA in violation of Cabinet decision resulting
in unjustified payment of 75.96 crore to DIMTS
After the approval of the Cabinet in November 2007, GNCTD appointed the
DIMTS as IM and a formal agreement was signed(April 2011) i.e. after lapse
of 40 months. As per Cabinet decision of September 2008, DIMTS was to be
paid three per cent of gross revenue from the operators including fare box and
advertising revenue to meet the cost associated towards providing service as
IM. However, the DOT entered into IMA, whose Article 6 as amended (May
2011) stated that ‘the DOT shall pay to the DIMTS a fee of% 50 lakh per
month or such other amount as may be decided by the Committee constituted
in pursuance of Cabinet decision (September 2008) or three per cent of fare-
box in the month or as amended by the Government from time to time,

71


Audit Report No. 3 of the year 2013

whichever is higher’. Scrutiny of the records revealed that the DIMTS was
paid an amount of = 7.19 crore (inclusive of service tax) for the period May
2011 to May 2012. As against this, considering the fare box collection
of 37.04 crore for the same period, actual IM fee at three per cent worked out
only to 71.23 crore. Thus, the DOT incurred an unjustified payment of 75.96
crore by violating the decision of the Cabinet.

The DOT stated (November 2012 and March 2013) that the determination of
IM fee as fixed percentage of the gross receipts was premised upon estimates
of receipts under the scheme in its totality i.e., with a full roll out of buses in
all 17 clusters. It further stated that = 50 lakh was the minimum amount to
sustain IM activities till critical mass of 1000 buses was being inducted in
phases. It, further, added that it had put up a revised fee structure to the
Committee of CS and followed its decision.

The reply was not acceptable, as financial prudence was not exercised while
dealing with the DIMTS, which was a separate entity and working to achieve
its own profits. Further, the Committee of CS in its meeting of November
2011 asked DIMTS to reassess its actual cost and decided that the DOT may
continue with the existing arrangement subject to adjustment of payments in
due course. It was only in meeting held in May 2012 that it was decided that
fee of IM should not be linked with the fare box collection and that the
DIMTS should propose a revised graded structure of fee linked to number of
buses in operation. Thus, the fact remains that the DOT made unjustified
payment of हैं 5.96 crore as IM fee to the DIMTS for May 2011 to March
2012.

(vii) Automatic Vehicle Location Tracking and Monitoring with Passenger
Information System (AVIMPS) Project

Pursuant to the order of the Delhi High Court (September 2007), DOT
decided that DTC would implement the Global Positioning System enabled
AVTMPS for its own buses as well as the cluster buses and the DIMTS would
provide technical support to the DOT in terms of required specification of the
hardware, software and vetting of RFP document prepared by DTC at a fee
mutually agreed to.

It was also decided that DIMTS may be appointed as bid process manager for
selecting vendor for the project at a onetime fee of रे 50 lakh and as technical
advisor and supervisory agency for Traffic Control Centre at a monthly fee of
% 36 lakh, to be subsumed in the fee of three per cent of gross revenue when it
comes in place as IM. However, the DOT asked the DIMTS (June 2009 ie.
within two weeks of the earlier decision that the project be undertaken by the
DTC) to submit their proposal for implementation wherein requirement of
accountability would have to be accepted by DIMTS. It was decided in the
meeting held under the chairmanship of Chief Minister of Delhi in July 2009
that the DIMTS would undertake setting up of AVTMPS/GPS and Passenger
Information System and order was passed by the DOT whereby, the DIMTS
would, besides DTC private cluster buses, execute the project for government
departments as well as autos and taxis. Accordingly, an agreement was signed
with DIMTS in March 2010.

72


Chapter-4 : CCO Based Audit

The DOT stated (March 2013) that earlier the DTC initiated a project for
implementation of GPS system in its buses on PPP model but tender of the
DTC did not materialize as the vendor did not honor the contract and his
security was forfeited by the DTC.

The reply of the DOT is not acceptable. After back out by the vendor, the
DIMTS submitted its proposals for setting up of GPS/AVTMPS project
(November 2008 and March 2009) to DOT. The matter was discussed in
DOT on May 4, 2009 as DIMTS was not willing to take the responsibility for
smooth functioning of various activities related to effective and efficient
implementation of the system. Therefore, DOT again decided to get the
project implemented by DTC with the concurrence of Finance Department
(May 2009). However, contrary to this, project was awarded to DIMTS for
implementation in June 2009.

Further observations on AVIMPS Project are discussed in subsequent
paragraphs:
(a) Appointment of Consultant as Implementing Agency

Scrutiny revealed that the DOT had obtained Finance Department’s
concurrence (May 2009) for the project to be undertaken by DTC, whereas the
project was awarded tothe DIMTS. The DOT awarded the project on
nomination basis with out a comprehensive plan and due diligence required
for such a major technical project with long term financial implications and
without ensuring commensurate and assured service delivery as well. DIMTS
was engaged as Project Management Consultant (PMC) even though the
terms and conditions dictated by it were not acceptable to the DOT. DIMTS
was not only the PMC, but also the project implementation agency, which was
in violation of CVC order’.

(b) Vetting of the Automatic Vehicle Location System (AVLS) agreement

Scrutiny revealed that citing urgency AVLS agreement was not sent to
Finance Department for yetting. The contention of urgency was not justified
as draft AVLS agreement was reccived by the DOT almost one month before
the date of AVLS agreement. In any case, signing of the agreement without
concurrence of the Finance Department was irregular.

The DOT stated (November 2012 and March 2013) that proposal of AVLS
was approved by the Finance Department and agreement was vetted by the
Law Department. The entailed payments were being released to project fund
through Finance Department. The DOT further stated that the Council of
Ministers in its meeting held on June 01, 2009 inter alia discussed the
implementation of GPS in buses and directed that the project would be
implemented by the DIMTS and not by the DTC. The meeting was attended
to by the then Pr, Secretary-cum-Commissioner (Transport) and he
accordingly put up a note in this regard for the approval of the Chief
Secretary/Cabinet Secretary to the Cabinet.

The reply was not acceptable as PAO withheld payment to the AVLS Project
Fund due to the fact that DOT had obtained Finance Department’s
concurrence (May 2009) for the project to be undertaken by DTC, whereas the

Audit Report No. 3 of the year 2013

project was being implemented by DIMTS. The DOT placed (February 2012)
a draft revised sanction for consideration of the Finance Department, which
allowed continuing with the present arrangement subject to approval from the
Minister of Transport and post-facto ratification from the Cabinet, if required.
However, such approvals were not found on record. Hence, contention that
the payments were being released to the Project Fund through the Finance
Department was not acceptable. The reply on the Cabinet decision of 01 June
2009 and corresponding note of the Pr. Secretary-cum-Commissioner
(Transport), approved by the Chief Secretary also, could not be corroborated
for the fact that the Cabinet decision 1533 dated 01 June 2009 did not mention
about implementation of AVTMPS by DIMTS. However, the DOT went
ahead with the appointment of DIMTS as implementer of the project.

(c) Parking of government funds outside Government Account

System of AVLS Project account being operated by the DIMTS,
involveskeeping substantial government fund outside Government Accounts
which was not in conformity with the provisions available under the General
Financial Rules.

The DOT stated (November 2012 and March 2013) that CS Committee
meeting of December 2011 specifically dealt with release of fund to the
AVLS Project Account and that concurrence from the Finance Department for
the financial commitment was obtained.

It further stated that GFRs also permit advances to be given to entities. The
placing of funds in advance with the DIMTS was on an analogy with the
procedure followed for deposit works and DIMTS spent funds in accordance
with Rule 215(3) of GFRs where it had been laid down that the funds for such
projects or schemes may be released in one or more installments.

The reply was not acceptable as the issues regarding concurrence of the
Finance Department to operation and procedure of DA and Project Account
remained unresolved in the meetings of the Chief Secretary Committee held in
November 2011 and May 2012.

Further, the ‘Deposit Works’ is applied to works of constructions or repairs of
building and mechanical/electrical equipments, the cost of which is met out of
government grants to autonomous bodies or institutions through their
administrative ministries. Rule 215 (3) of GFR addresses the schemes or
projects relating to creation of assets and that too when schemes are to be
undertaken by autonomous organizations, and the DIMTS was not an
autonomous body.

(d) Service Delivery and Service Level Agreement management charges

The charges for ‘Service Delivery and SLA Management’ of the activities of
the GPS Vendor/GPRS vendor as approved and agreed to by the DIMTS in
the meeting of Committee of CS, held in December 2009, had not been
approved by the Finance Department. The DOT paid? 2.94 crore for the
period from 03 March 2010 to 30 November 2011 as ‘Service Delivery and
SLA Management’ charges to the DIMTS which was, thus, irregular.

The DOT stated (March 2013) that DIMTS had been informed vide letter
dated 17 February 2010 that the Finance Department had concurred to the

74


Chapter-4 : CCO Based Audit

payment of = 12 lakh plus service tax per month as service delivery and SLA
Management charges to the DIMTS.

The reply was not acceptable as the DOT had not stated upfront whether
approval of the Finance Department was obtained. Further, no supporting
evidence was observed by audit.

(e) Backend management charge

The DIMTS was appointed as technical advisor and supervisory agency for
Traffic Control Centre at a monthly fee of हैं 36 lakh to be subsumed in the IM
fee of three per cent of gross revenue when the DIMTS would come in place
as IM. Audit scrutiny revealed that the DIMTS as IM came in place in April

Management Charges of % 36 lakh (as per AVLS agreement), even after the
IM agreement. An amount of % 3.11 crore was paid to the DIMTS as Backend
Management charges for the period from 06 April 2011 to 30 November
2011, without it being subsumed in the IM fee, which was, irregular and
avoidable expenditure.

The DOT stated (March 2013)that as per Article 6 of IM Agreement backend
management service charges for AVLS would be deducted from IM Fees,
once the Cluster fleet reaches critical mass level of 1000 buses. There is no
increase in fees based on escalation stipulated in the Agreement since CS
Committee meeting dated 15.5,2012 decided that fixed charges shall be paid.

The reply failed to address the audit contention that the Finance Department
approved appointinent of the DIMTS for backend management charges at a
fee of हैं 36 lakhs per month till the system of IM was in place, when the said
fee was to be subsumed in the IM fee.

(ii) Unjustified expenditure of 215.62 lakh for software updation

M/s ESP was issuing the vehicle test report in FORM 38 1.९., the prescribed
form of Certificate of Fitness (COF). This created confusion among the public
as they took their vehicle on road without obtaining appropriate COF issued
by the Board of Inspectors of the Department. Observing this, DOT asked M/s
ESP India Pvt. Ltd. to issue test report in the requisite format. For this, M/s
ESP India Pvt. Ltd. modified the software in April 2008 and raised invoice for
र₹ 15.62 lakh (July 2008) and the DOT paid the amount in March 2009. Audit
observed that as per Clause 2.4.1 (0) of the agreement between DOT and M/s
ESP India Pvt. Ltd., the vendor was to provide format of the test report as
required by the DOT. Thus, the payment of हैं 15.62 lakh to M/s ESP India
Pvt. Ltd. for modification in the software was unjustified.

The DOT stated (March 2013) that the software was designed in such a
manner that it generates fitness certificate in Form 38 and it was necessary to
modify the software so that M/s ESP India Pvt. Ltd. would issue only test
report.

The reply was not acceptable as M/s ESP India Pvt. Ltd. in its proposal had
also mentioned generation of test reports. Further,for generation of test report,
no new data/ information was required, hence, no modification/ updation of
the software.

(iii) Unreliable certificate of fitness (COF)

M/s ESP India Pvt. Ltd. was to conduct various tests on vehicles before
declaring it qualified for getting COF. The throughput of the test lanes was 18
to 22 vehicles per pit in eight hours. As such, at the most, 33 vehicles could
be tested in 12 hours per day per pit, meaning 99 vehicles on three pits. A test
check of the test statistics for April 2010 to March 2011, revealed that M/s
ESP tested 300 to 459 vehicles per day on three pits (Annexure+4.3).
Similarly, the operator required 8-15 minutes for conducting Third Party
Technical Inspection (TPTI) on a Compressed Natural Gas (CNG) driven
vehicle i.e. at most eight vehicles can be tested in an hour on one lane.
However, it was observed that during the month of March 2012, M/s ESP
IndiaPvt. Ltd.had tested, on several occasions, more than 16 vehicles in one
hour on one lane (Bay No. 8)

Thus, clearance of vehicles in excess of pit’s capacity, indicated compromise
with the prescribed tests and issue of unreliable COFs.

The DOT stated (March 2013) that tests were conducted on automated tests
lanes, which were fully computer controlled and TPTI was simultaneously
conducted at the same time. It further stated that they worked beyond working

79


Audit Report No. 3 of the year 2013

hours to clear all the vehicles who deposited fee on given day to prevent
backlog.

The reply was not acceptable as throughput of the test lanes was 18 to 22
vehicles per pit in eight hours andAudit had observed instances where the
operator cleared as many as 26 vehicles per hour.

(ii) Implementation of Third Party Technical Inspection (TPTI) Unit for

testing of CNG driven vehicles

The Ministry of Road Transport and Highways (MORTH) stipulated

medium, light, and two/three wheeler vehicles respectively) directly from the

(a) Unauthorised levy of fee for TPTI

Section 64 of the Motor Vehicles Act, 1988 stipulates that the Central
Government may make rules related to Certificate of Fitness (COF) with
regard to its form, period of effectiveness and the fee to be charged. As per
Rule 81 of Central Motor Vehicles Rules, 1989 (CMVR), the fee chargeable
for conducting test of a vehicle for grant and renewal of certificate of fitness
for two/three-wheeled vehicle, light motor vehicle, medium motor vehicle,
and heavy motor vehicle is 7100, 200, 300, and %400 respectively.

Audit observed that the Department has no authority under the Act to levy/
modify fee chargeable for COF. It was also observed that M/s ESP India Pvt.
Ltd. had not installed four setups of loaded mode emission systems at
Inspection and Certification (I&C) test lanes. Thus, the fee charged from
vehicle ownersin excess of the fee chargeable under the Motor Vehicle Act

80

was unauthorized burden on vehicle owners and resulted in undue benefits to
the vendor

The DOT stated (March 2013) that Section 111 (d) of MV Act 1988 stipulated
the power of State Government to make rules for “periodical testing and
inspection of vehicles by prescribed authorities?’ Further, Rule 98 of Delhi
Motor Vehicle Rules 1993, stipulates - “The periodical testing and inspection
of vehicles shall be conducted according to the methods prescribed in this
regards by the Commissioner (Transport) from time to time.”

(iii) Procurement of Remote Sensing Device (RSD) and contract for their
operation and maintenance

In July 2010, the DOT proposed for induction of “Remote Sensing Device”
for pollution checking of moving vehicles at all major entry points of Delhi
The Department of Planning recommended (September 2010) only one RSD
for a pilot project on Build Own Operate and Transfer (BOOT) basis as the
DOT did not have wherewithal to operate and maintain RSD. However, the
DOT proposed for procurement of 19 RSDs on direct or hire purchase basis.
The DOT prepared global tender on the basis of tender of West Bengal (who
were also in the process of procurement of RSD) and floated it in May 2011
for supply, operation and maintenance of RSD, on outright purchase or BOOT
basis. It incurred an expenditure of%22.07 lakh on advertisement but received
only single bid for the project. The proposed total outlay for the project for 10
years would be %245.41 crore.

The DOT stated (March 2013) that tll date no RSD equipment had been
purchased and bid had been annulled. It further stated that ARAI was a
statutory testing agency under Rule 126 of CMVR, 1989 and the global tender
was floated with the condition of approval of RSD technology from ARAI.
The reply was not acceptable as Rule 126 of CMVR, 1989 stipulates that
every manufacturer or importer of motor vehicles other than trailers and semi-
trailers should submit the prototype of the vehicle to be manufactured or
imported by him for test by various agencies mentioned therein. Audit
observed that the rule did not stipulate that RSD had to be approved by ARAI.
(a) Deviation from Cabinet decision

It was seen that the Cabinet approved (December 2007) opening of adequate
number of Pollution Under Control (PUC)centres at border points of Delhi
and refused its approval for the State to decide on pollution testing
procedures. Thus, adoption of RSD for pollution testing was in deviation of
the Cabinet decision to that extent.

The DOT stated (March 2013) that proposal of RSD was not started from the
Cabinet decision. It further stated that RSD was found to be more suitable
than the conventional equipment.

The reply was not acceptable asin the context of pollution check with RSD,
ARAI had clarified (March 2009) that “the system cannot replace
conventional Inspection and Certification (1 & C) techniques but can
complement the system. Secondly, there is no direct correlation between

81


Audit Report No. 3 of the year 2013

conventional I & C and RSD data”. ARAI further clarified that the efficacy of
RSD for checking air pollution for two-wheelers was not established for
which the capture rate was only 30per cent during trial run.

(b) Planning

The DOT conceptualized the project in January 2009. The ARAI
recommended carrying out a pilot project using RSD for six to 12 months
before implementing the technology in Indian scenario. International Centre
for Automotive Technology (iCAT) also suggested for trial runs before
adopting RSD. After conducting trial runs for about seven days in August
2009, the DOT went ahead (January 2012) for outright purchase of 19 RSDs at
a likely cost of = 37.81 crore and %207.60 crore for operation and maintenance
respectively for 10 years against the advice of Planning Department for
purchase of only one RSD as pilot project.

The DOT wasted considerable administrative efforts and time which could
have been utilised to gain sufficient knowledge about the new technology by
using various methods viz. discussion with the stakeholders, inviting
Expression of Interest (EOI), or implementing pilot project etc. It was
seen that the Remote Sensing Device could not replace conventional
Inspection & Certification (I&C) techniques, as adopted in PUC centres. It
was neither legally sufficient in terms of the Central Motor Vehicles Rules’,
nor could its data be correlated with conventional 1&C data. The system was
also unreliable in the multi lane roads, two wheelers and was dependent on
ambient conditions.

Further the tender bid invited by of Government of West Bengal was on
BOOT basis for a period of three years for one RSD with likely expenditure
of only रें 6.75 lakh per month for 36 months, including cost of equipment and
operation and maintenance which was substantially lower than the cost for
DOT.

The DOT stated (March 2013) that the technology was to identify the gross
polluter and issuance of notices/direction under rule 116 of CMVR, 1989.

The reply was not tenable as the challans based on RSD tests would not be
legally effective because as per sub Rule (3) of Rule 116 of the Central Motor
Vehicles Rules, 1989, the measurement for smoke emission level and Carbon
Monoxide level for vehicles etc. should be done with a meter approved by any
agency referred to in Rule 126 of the principal rules or by the National
Environmental Engineering Research Institute, Nagpur, provided that such a
testing agency follows ISO or ECE Standards and procedures for approval of
measuring meters

The objective of the scheme is to make relevant data/information available to
know whether new mode of transport or modification of existing projects is
feasible and if so, whether it is financially viable and what social benefits shall
be derived there from.

5.5 Decongestion and rationalization of ISBTs, development of bus
terminals anddepots and loan to Delhi Transport Infrastructure

Development Corporation(DTIDC)

In August 2010, GNCTD formed a wholly owned separate legal entity,
namely, DTIDC, under the Companies Act, 1956 with financial and
administrative autonomy to cater to the ever increasing need of operation,
management and maintenance of ISBTs, bus stands and depots, besides taking
entire responsibility of creation, development, financing and maintenance of
transport infrastructure.

The three schemes -‘Decongestion and Rationalisation of Inter State Bus
Terminals (ISBTs)’, ‘Development of Bus Terminals and Depots’ and ‘Loan
to DTIDC’ were selected for audit. Their objectives are to tackle the inter-
state passenger/commercial traffic so that citizens residing at the outskirts of
Delhi are not made to do much travel to get the buses to desired destinations,
to check air pollution due to exhaust of the diesel buses of other states
entering into and leaving Delhi, to reduce extra load from the Kashmere Gate
ISBT and to outsource construction of new ISBTS, subject to availability of
land at suitable sites,to develop bus terminals and depots at suitable sites in
Delhi to improve the services of DTC which in turn would help in enhancing
the convenience of the commuters. An amount of हैं 60.00 crore was
sanctioned to DTIDC for taking up the development as well as meeting out of
expenses for development of ISBTs.

The audit findings are discussed in subsequent paragraphs.

(i) Unauthorized payment of €48.39 crore to DIMTS for renovation and
upgradation of ISBT, Kashmere Gate

Sealed bids were invited by DIMTS(December 2009) for upgradation and
renovation of ISBT at Kashmere Gate. The DOT accepted the tender of M/s
Nagarjuna Construction Company (M/s. NCC) in June 2010 at € 73.19 crore.
However, it was observed that the agreement between the DOT and the
DIMTS for project management consultancy was signed in June 2010. Thus,
inviting tenders by the DIMTS in December 2009 and their opening and
processing in February 2010 i.e. before signing any agreement with DOT, was
irregular.

83


Audit Report No. 3 of the year 2013

Scrutiny of records revealed that the DOT and the DTIDC had been making
payment to the DIMTS for the work executed by M/s. NCC, which was
irregular as the agreement for renovation of ISBT was between the DOT and
M/s. NCC. Furthermore, as there was no agreement between the DTIDC and
the NCC or the DTIDC and the DIMTS, the DTIDC was not authorized to
make any payment to M/s. NCC or the DIMTS.

The DOT and DTIDC had made a payment of हैं 47.50 crore to the DIMTS,
besides payment of % 6.75 crore towards Project Management Consultancy
(PMC) charges. The audit findings were as follows

(i) The DTIDC, presently making payment for renovation and
upgradation, has no control over the progress, workmanship, quality control
etc. As a result, the work, due to be completed by February 2011, was still
incomplete (August 2012). Only 66 per cent of work was completed, while
payment of % 47.50 crore had been made to the DIMTS. The DTIDC was in
no position to take any action against the NCC or the DIMTS as it was not a
party to the agreement. It was further not clear whether the final agreement, if
at all made, between the DOT and the DIMTS had the concurrence of the Law
Department of GNCTD.

Thus, the payment of % 47.50 crore made to the DIMTS upto July 2012, was
irregular.

ii) As per the agreement between the DOT and the DIMTS, the Project
Manager was entitled to a remuneration of five per centof actual cost of the
project. In addition, Project Manager was also entitled a fee of three per cent
of the minimum estimated cost/actual cost of the project for planning, design,
structural drawing and architects fee. Thus, the total fee payable to the DIMTS
works out to हैं 5.86 crore (8 per cent of 73.19 crore). But scrutiny of records
revealed that the DTIDC has already paid % 6.75 crore upto July 2012 to the
DIMTS on account of consultancy fee. Thus, DOT/ DTIDC has already paid
an excess remuneration of € 0.89 crore to the DIMTS.

The DOT stated (March 2013) that the Expenditure Finance Committee
(EFC) under the chairperson of Hon’ble Chief Minister in its meeting of April
5, 2010, inter-alia approved the Phase-1 of the renovation of ISBT, Kashmere
Gate at the successful bid amount of हैं 74.89 crore plus Project Manager’s
remuneration for DIMTS @ 5 per cent on the estimated cost of % 70.47 crore.
Payment was made to DIMTS in accordance to the Project Management
Consultancy agreement signed between Transport Department and DIMTS for
redevelopment/upgradation of ISBT, Kashmere Gate. The DOT further stated
that the agreement with NCC had been signed by the DOT. As a Project
Manager, it was the responsibility of DIMTS to ensure the progress of the
work including quality and workmanship and DTIDC would take necessary
action in respect of completion of work as per various clauses of the
concession agreement signed between Transport Department and NCC at the
time of grant of extension of time to the said contractor.

The reply of the DOT strengthens the audit observation that bids were invited
by DIMTS not only before signing of the agreement with the DOT, but even
before the EFC decision of April 5, 2010 to renovate the ISBT, Kashmere
Gate.

84


Chapter-4 : CCO Based Audit

The fact remains that the contract for redevelopment/upgradation was signed
between the DOT and M/s. NCC. Hence, making payment to M/s. NCC
through the DIMTS could not be justified. The reply was also not acceptable
as the work for upgradation/redevelopment was awarded by the DOT to M/S
NCC Ltd. Hence, payment to the contractor through DIMTS instead of being
made directly, could not be justified.

(ii) Irregular payment of € seven crore to the DIMTS for ISBT at Anand

Vihar and Sarai Kale Khan

‘The GNCTD appointed the DIMTS as Project Management Consultant for
redevelopment of ISBTs at Anand Vihar and Sarai Kale Khan and released
% seven crore ( two crore in April 2008 and @ five crore in October 2008).

Tt was seen that whereas the agreement for Project Management Consultancy
(PMC) work was signed between DoT and DIMTS in June 2010, हें seven
crore was released to DIMTS much earlier (€ two crore in April 2008 and
2 five crore in October 2008).

The DTIDC stated (September 2012) that the payment released to the DIMTS
before signing of PMC was as per the agreed terms and conditions and
approval of the competent authority. The reply was not acceptable because the
Cabinet decided in September 2008, whereas the amount of ~ 2 crore was
released to DIMTS in April 2008.

The DOT stated (March 2013) that the Council of Ministers vide Cabinet
decision No. 1467 dated 22 September 2008 had assigned the responsibility of
planning construction, project management operation, maintenance, design
and implementation of upgradation/redevelopment of ISBT at Anand Vihar
and Sarai Kale Khan to the DIMTS.

The fact remains that two crore was released (April 2008) to DIMTS even
before Cabinet decision for upgradation/redevelopment of ISBT at Anand
Vihar and Sarai Kale Khan.

(iii) Blockade of funds amounting to हैं. 20.36 crore in respect of
development of Bus Terminal at Vasant Kunj

The DDA allotted a piece of land measuring 38,900 sqm to the DOT for
construction of Bus Terminal or Depot at Vasant Kunj in January 2011 at a
provisional cost of = 20.36 crore. The payment was made by the DTC in
February 2011 on behalf of the DOT.

The physical inspection of the land was done by a team of officers from the
DDA, the DOT, the DTIDC and the DIMTS in May 2011 which observed that
the land was encroached upon by a temple, Nallah, taxi stand, and tube well
etc. Even then, the DOT took over the possession of the land in June 2011.
The DOT also came to know in March 2012 that the ownership of the land
was under litigation. The alternative land has not been received by the DOT,
even after 14 months (September 2012) of making payment of & 20.36 crore.

The DOT stated (March 2013) that Government was making all out efforts
with the DDA to free the encroachment at the earliest and demarcate the
portion of land, which was under litigation. The DOT further stated that it was
making sincere efforts to get additional land from the DDA. A final decision

85


Audit Report No. 3 of the year 2013

on the Vasant Kunj matter would be taken by the DOT in consultation with
the DDA to construct the bus depot in the free land or to get alternative land.

The reply did not contradict the audit observations.

Conclusions

The DOT is mandated to ensure appropriate transport solutions keeping in
view the increase in the population and traffic demands in Delhi apart from
providing interstate transport solutions. The DOT undertook 20 schemes
during the 117 Five Year Plan period. The DOT carried out most of its
technical functions through private organizations since it lacked capacity to
steer or even control and monitor them. The DOT appointed private entities
like the DIMTS or the ESP on nomination basis, in non-transparent and non-
competitive manner in violation of the codal provisions. It also favoured the
entities, by circumventing rules or outright violations of codal provisions,
cabinet decisions, guidelines, CVC orders and propriety. The DOT was
neither able to ensure delivery of its intended services nor safeguard the
interests of the Government or vehicle users, and ultimately discharging its
mandated role in transport sector in Delhi.

Recommendations

> The DOT may strengthen its budgetary control to ensure that expenditure
does not exceed the allocation,

> The DOT should follow government rules and procedures in assignment of
works/ consultancies/ studies to safe guard the public interest,

> An independent technical cellmay be created to evaluate the proposals
providing solutions to the traffic and transport related problems, and

> The department may develop a system of internal control and monitoring
commensurate with its size and diversified activities.

86


Chapter-4 : CCO Based Audit

4.2 CCO based audit on the role of the Department of Food,

Supplies and Consumer Affairs in managing supply of
essential commodities

= Summary

A Chief Controlling Officer (CCO) based audit of the Department of Food
Supplies and Consumer Affairs (the Department) was conducted to evaluate
its role in managing equitable distribution of essential commodities to the
intended beneficiaries at fair prices.

The Department was issuing ration cards to the beneficiaries under APL
(unstamped) category which were not provided SFAs and kerosene oil. No
realistic targets for identification of beneficiaries were adopted by the
Department. Even after a lapse of more than 12 years, it was relying on
figures projected by the Gol in March 2000 and no notification was issued
during the year for issuing new cards.

The system of issuance, renewal/review and amendments of ration cards was
deficient as no documents were being preserved by the Department in order to
justify disbursement of SFAs to actual beneficiaries as per their entitled
category.

Allocated quantities by the Gol could not be utilised in full by the Department
due to unrealistic demand of SFAs projected by it in terms of number of
beneficiaries.

Existence of FPSs/KODs in excess of norms in terms of number of
beneficiaries, inadequate retailer margin and irregular enhancement of
transportation charges at the cost of FPSs and beneficiaries coupled with non-
follow-up of policy decisions to improve the financial returns, led to unviable
operating conditions, thereby increasing the risk of FPS/KOD owners
resorting to unfair means or closing down the business.

Non-implementation of revised policy on distribution of wheat flour to curb
leakages of food grains under the TPDS, inadequate monitoring of FPS and
inadequacies in complaints redressal mechanism reflected weak internal
control mechanism prevailing in the Department.

The Department was performing insignificant activities under open market
interventions in order to arrest inflationary trend in cases of abrupt price rise
of certain essential commodities. In the absence of the database of existing
wholesalers, millers and retailers to operationalize its mechanism of open
market sale scheme, the Department was not in a position to check potential
black-marketing practices and stabilize prices of essential commodities when
required.

87


Audit Report No. 3 of the year 2013

4.2.1 Introduction

The Department of Food, Supplies and Consumer Affairs (the Department)
was established in 1962 to undertake Public Distribution and management of
trade and commerce in essential commodities. It intends to secure equitable
distribution and availability of essential commodities at fair prices by
enforcing the Essential Commodities Act, 1955 and various Control Orders
made there under.

The Department indents for monthly allocation of Specified Food Articles
(SFA) to the Food Corporation of India (FCI) and authorizes the Delhi State
Civil Supplies Corporation (DSCSC) to lift and deliver the same to the
respective Fair Price Shop (FPS) for further distribution to various categories
of beneficiaries. FPSs, in turn, are required to deposit the amount relating to
the cost of SFAs and transportation charges in advance in the bank account of
the DSCSC for their monthly allocation. The DSCSC pays the amount of
SFAs to FCI at the time of lifting and retains the amount of transportation
charges. The Department also undertakes activities in respect of Open Market
operation to ensure equitable distribution and availability of essential
commodities at fair prices.

As against the sanctioned strength of 1142 posts, 624 persons were in position
as of March 2012, with 518 posts lying vacant. There are 22 DDOs in the
Department, out of which 10 deal with the activities relating to the food and
supply.

4.2.1.1 Schemes undertaken by the Department

The Department implements following schemes:
Centrally Sponsored Scheme (CSS)

(i) Consumer Awareness Programme

(ii) Annapurna Scheme

State Sponsored Scheme (SSS)

(i) Annapurna Scheme (State Share)

4.2.1.2 Organizational structure

The Department is headed by the Secretary-cum-Commissioner assisted by a
Special Secretary-cum-Special Commissioner and two Additional
Commissioners. An Assistant Commissioner heads each of the nine Zones
which are further divided into 70 Circles (one for each assembly constituency)
each headed by a Food Supply Officer.

No laid down Office Procedure was adhered to at any level of hierarchy in the

A three tier system viz. head office, zonal office and circle office exists in the

to fraud and corruption

The distribution of SFAs and kerosene oil involves issues relating to
timeliness, quality and quantity as well as identification of eligible
beneficiaries. Inadequate number of inspections of PDS outlets conducted by
the Department, non-renewal and review of beneficiaries in order to eliminate

It could be seen from the table that even though resources were available for
implementation of CSS (Central as well as State share), they have not been
utilized for the same.

According to Appendix 2 of Rule 48 of the General Financial Rules,
Ministries/Departments are required to prepare their estimates. keeping in
view the trends of disbursements during the previous years and other relevant
factors like instructions on economy issued by the Ministry of Finance, from
time to time. Rule 56 (2) further stipulates that savings as well as provisions
that cannot be profitably utilized, should be surrendered to Government as
soon as these are foreseen, without waiting till the end of the year. The
objective is to minimize the scope for avoiding surrenders at a later stage.
Financial management analysis of the Department revealed mismanagement
with respect to planning of activities/expenditure in order to utilize the
allocated funds as elaborated in the subsequent paragraphs.

The Department, in its reply (March 2013), stated that the funds under the
Annapurna Scheme were allotted in year 2001, but as no eligible claimant
could be located, the same were not utilized and were being surrendered.
Reply of the Department was not acceptable, as it neither planned its activities

92


Chapter-4 : CCO Based Audit

to identify the beneficiaries under the scheme, nor surrendered the funds
which were lying unutilized for more than a decade. Moreover, the
Department did not furnish reasons for non-utilization of funds amounting to
र₹ 17.30 lakh allotted under the Consumer Awareness Programme.

Scrutiny of budgetary statements for the years 2010-11 and 2011-12 revealed
that there were persistent savings of हैं 2.50 crore under the head 3456 D.1 (4)
(5) - Provision for Market Intervention to check the rising prices of Essential
Commodities, which was 100 per cent of the total appropriation. The
Department, in its reply (November 2012), justified savings by referring to its
surrender letter (March 2012). It further stated (March 2013) that the fund was
preserved as contingency fund to deal with emergent situation of price rise
and the same would be utilized only after issuing necessary guidelines by the
GNCTD, as per the decision of the Cabinet, in case any particular commodity
had an unprecedented price rise, generating public resentment. Reply of the
Department was not acceptable as it did not utilize allocated funds to
undertake the said activity to fulfill its mandate to secure availability of
essential commodities during 2010-11 and 2011-12, even though there were
instances of steep price rise during this period.
