4.2.7 Compliance/Propriety Audit

4.2.7.1 Suspected enhancement of transportation charges without
eking concurrence of the Finance Department

Rule 26 of the Transaction of Business of the Government of National Capital
Territory of Delhi Rules, 1993 provides that the Finance Department shall be
consulted in all matters in which its previous concurrence is necessary under
these rules. Further, Rule 18 (1) stipulates that when the subject of any
proposal or matter relates to more than one department, no order shall be
issued (nor shall the proposal be placed before the Council) till it has been
considered by all the Departments concerned, unless the proposal is one of
extreme urgency.

As per the view submitted by the Finance (Accounts) Department of GNCTD,
the revision of issue price of SFAs is a policy matter and approval of Principal
Secretary (Finance) is required.It was, however, noticed that approval for
enhancement of retail prices of Sugar was obtained (September 2011) from
the Lieutenant Governor of Delhi bypassing the Finance Department. Thus,
an increase in the retail issue price of the Sugar by 15 paise per kg was made
on account of revision of transportation charges. The Department was
requested to furnish documentary evidence in respect of concurrence of the

96


Chapter-4 : CCO Based Audit

Finance Department to the enhancement imposing an additional burden of %
11.67 lakh on the BPL beneficiaries for the period from November 2011 to
March 2012.

In its reply (March 2013) the Department stated that the proposal was
approved by the Lt. Governor of Delhi and the matter was being processed for
ex-post-facto approval of the Finance Department which was missed since the
proposal did not have any impact on exchequer. Reply of the Department was
not acceptable as impact of enhancement of the transportation charges was
recurting in nature and imposed as an additional burden on the already
economically weaker BPL beneficiaries, defying its mandate of providing
essential commodities at fair prices under PDS.

4.2.8 Review of operational performance of the Department

As per the Citizen Charter of the Department (2011), the entire population of
NCTD is divided in the following categories for the purpose of proper supply
and distribution of SFAs and Kerosene oil:

. Above Poverty Line (APL) - Families having total family income
above Zone lakh per annum are issued unstamped APL cards and
below Zone lakh and above हैं 24200 per annum are issued stamped
APL cards (APL(S)). Beneficiaries of APL (S) cards only are eligible
for SFAs (25 kgs of wheat and 10 kgs of rice) and kerosene oil (9.5
liters).

° Below Poverty Line (BPL) - Families having total family income
below हैं 24200 per annum are identified under this category and
entitled to SFAs and Kerosene oil @ 25 kgs wheat, 10 kgs rice, six kgs
sugar and 9.5 litres kerosene oil at subsidized rates.

. Antyodaya Anna Yojna (AAY)- Household with disabled adult and
no assured means of subsistence, land less labourers, marginal farmers,
artisans, craftsmen, widows, terminally ill persons, etc. are covered
under this scheme. They are entitled for SFAs and Kerosene oil @ 25
kgs wheat, 10 kgs rice, six kgs sugar and 9.5 litres kerosene oil.

. Annapurna Yojna (AY) - Under this scheme, destitute persons of
more than 65 years of age, having no regular source of income and not
availing benefit of National Old Age Pension or State Pension Scheme
are entitled for 10 Kg. of foodgrains free of cost every month,

. Allotment of food grains to Welfare Institutions- The Welfare
Institutions recognised by the Department of Social Welfare, such as,
Beggars’ Homes, Nari Niketan, Hostels for SC/STs and Backward
Class students, Homes for street children, destitutes, neglected,
delinquent and abandoned children, juveniles, old persons, etc. are
supplied food grains @ 15kg. per inmate per month at the rate
prescribed for BPL.

In addition to above, SFAs (25 kgs of wheat and 10 kgs of rice) and kerosene
oil (9.5 liters) are also disbursed to the beneficiaries of the Jhnuggi Ration Card
(JRC) holders at the rate prescribed for APL.

Audit Report No. 3 of the year 2013

Actual quantity of SFAs and kerosene oil to be disbursed during the year and
rates to be charged from various categories of beneficiaries, however, vary on
the basis of the notification issued by the Government of India (Gol) from
time to time.

As on 31 March 2012, there were 1585313 APL (unstamped) cards/

revealed that new ration cards were being issued only in respect of APL
(unstamped) category. Further, as per the policy, no SFAs and Kerosene oil is
being distributed to cardholders of the APL (unstamped) category. The
Department was pursuing to issue ration cards to this category of beneficiaries
to whom it was not providing any relief in terms of distribution of SFAs. The
ration cards so issued are valid only for the purpose of procurement of SFAs
and kerosene oil and not for any other purposes such as proof of identity, etc.
Thus, engagement of the Department in this activity was unreasonable.

In its reply (March 2013), the Department attributed this activity to past
practice, as ration card was used as family identity since last many years. The
Departinent further justified its act by stating that earlier it had distributed the
surplus allocation in January, February, July and September 2010 and that the
issue needed a policy decision at the level of the GNCTD. Reply of the
Department was not tenable in terms of the PDS (Control) Order, 2001, which
provided that the ration card should not be used as the document of identity.
Further, the contention of the Department that surplus allocation was
distributed among APL (Unstamped) beneficiaries, also did not hold good as
the Department had itself excluded the APL (unstamped) category cards from
the projection it made during the current year.

Gol decided (August 2001) to give flexibility to the State Governments/UT
Administrations in the matter of fixing the margin for the FPS owner by
removing the restriction of keeping the difference between the retail issue
price and the Central Issue Price (CIP) of food grains for distribution to BPL
families under Targeted Public Distribution System (TPDS) within 50 paise
per kg. Thus, the relaxation was available only for revision of retail issue price
on account of fixing the margin for the FPS owner and not for revision of
transportation charges payable to DSCSC.

However, scrutiny of records revealed that the Department increased
transportation charges and sale price of SFAs w.e.f. 1 November 2011. The
transportation charges in respect of supply of Wheat and Rice for BPL
category were revised from % 15 per quintal to हैं 30 per quintal resulting in
increase of end retail price to 65 paise per kg for the BPL category

BPL beneficiaries would be reviewed in terms of Gol directions as pointed out
by audit.

Chapter-4 : CCO Based Audit

(a) Lapses in the system of identification of beneficiaries

(i) In terms of Clause 2 (1) of Annexe to the PDS (Control) Order, 2001,
State Governments shall ensure that no eligible applicant is denied a ration
card under the PDS. It was, however, observed that no notification was issued.
by the Department for issuing of new cards to BPL category after March
2009. In fact, no realistic revised and fresh targets for identification of
beneficiaries were adopted by the Department. Even after a lapse of more than
12 years, it was relying on figures as projected by the Gol in March 2000,
according to which the number of BPL households in Delhi was 4.09 lakh.
The Department intimated (July 2012) a deficit of 44739 cards for BPL/AAY
category in comparison to the targets being pursued by it. The Department
failed to furnish details of targets fixed with regard to the other category of
cardholders as mentioned above. Absence of any realistic and updated targets
with regard to identification of beneficiaries reflected failure and passive role
of the Department in discharging its mandated responsibilities.

(ii) In a meeting held in May 2011 under the Chairmanship of the Minister
(Power, Development and Food & Civil Supplies), it was desired to devise a
procedure to invite applications only from the vulnerable section among BPL
like aged, handicapped persons, so that these persons could be issued BPL
cards, as inviting applications from all eligible BPL families would open flood
of applications, whereas the available vacancies were around 54,000 keeping
in view the target of 4.09 lakh BPL cards fixed by the Gol in March
2000.Similar practice had been adopted by the Department during the
conversion of BPL cards into AAY cards during 2007 also, where instructions
were issued for not converting BPL cards to AAY cards, as target of AAY
cards in Delhi had since been achieved. As of March 2012, there were
approximate 1.03 lakh AAY ration cards only. Since a proper system of food
Management entails a systematic issuance of ration cards to the eligible
applicants on regular basis, inaction on the part of the Department to evolve
such a system led to denial of ration cards to the eligible and needy residents
of Delhi. Audit, therefore, could not ensure that the actual numbers of
existing beneficiaries under various categories are the only eligible
beneficiaries in Delhi under the PDS system.

In its reply (March 2013), the Department stated that despite repeated request
by the GNCTD, the Planning Commission and Gol did not allow increase in
the ceiling. Moreover, shortfall in the number of cards issued to BPL/AAY
category was attributed to non-review of 24500 old BPL cards and
approximately 14200 new fresh BPL cards not collected by applicants and
accordingly eliminated. The Department also stated that it was reviewing the
inactive category of APL/BPL cards which were reintroduced in the active
category after due verification and approval. Reply of the Department was not
tenable as even within the ceiling prescribed by the Gol, the Departinent did
not make any effort, annually, to weed out bogus and ineligible cards, so that
it was not forced to deny cards to genuine beneficiaries.

99


Audit Report No. 3 of the year 2013

Chapter-4 : CCO Based Audit

4.2.9.2 Non-updating of data

As per clause 2(8) of the Public Distribution System (Control) Order, 2001,
elimination of bogus cards as well as bogus units in the ration cards shall be a
continuous exercise by the State Government to check diversion of essential
commodities. For this purpose, the State Government shall get the lists of
BPL and Antyodaya families reviewed every year, for updating the data of
eligible and ineligible families. Further, a ration card shall be valid for a
period of five years from the date of its issue unless it is suspended or
cancelled earlier. A ration card shall be issued afresh or renewed after fresh
verification of antecedents and such other checks, as may be prescribed by the
State Government in this regard.

Non-adherence to the provisions of the PDS (Control) Order, 2001, in respect
of review of BPL and AAY cards every year, was pointed out in the Report of
the C & AG for the year ended March 2011 (Para 2.1.7.5). However, no
initiative had been taken by the Department to evolve such a system during
the period 2011-12.

In its reply (July 2012), the Department admitted non-revision of BPL and
AAY cards during the year 2011-12 and stated that the cards had been
reviewed biometrically in the year 2009-10. Reply of the Department is not
acceptable as it had failed to adhere to the provisions stipulated under the PDS
(Control) Order, 2001 and that reviewing of cards biometrically would only
ensure physical existence of the beneficiary in Delhi without verifying the
eligibility of the beneficiary in his present category on the basis of proof of
income. No reply was furnished with regard to renewal of cards.

It was further seen that no caution had been exercised by the Department
while authorising amendments and issuing duplicate ration cards, as the
beneficiaries had been issued amended/duplicate ration cards irrespective of
annual income declared afresh by them, thereby, losing an opportunity to
revise the ration cards as per their present socio-economic status under various
categories

Scrutiny of 452 application forms in selected Circle offices revealed that in 72
cases, proof/declaration of annual income was not insisted from beneficiaries
for the purpose of amendment and issuance of duplicate ration cards. Cases
were noticed where the duplicate cards were issued on the basis of FIRs but
the number of the duplicate ration cards were different than those mentioned
in FIR. Cases were also noticed where the duplicate cards were issued on the
basis of incomplete forms submitted by the applicants (Annexure-4.9)
Irregularities in processing applications and ignoring the present financial
status of the beneficiaries may lead to distribution of SFAs, kerosene oil and
extension of subsidy to the ineligible people.

The Department stated (March 2013) that there was no such provision to ask
for income certificate at intermediate levels or in case of issuance of duplicate
ration cards on account of damage/loss/theft etc. Tt was further stated that
detailed guidelines would be reiterated to enforce the existing provisions and
Zonal ACs directed to enforce the provisions of PDS (Control) Order, 2001
Reply of the Department was not acceptable as it did not capitalize on the
opportunity to verify the antecedents of the beneficiaries at the time of issuing

101


Audit Report No. 3 of the year 2013

of amended/duplicate cards in the light of its failure to review the cards every
year to update the data of eligible and ineligible beneficiaries.

The Department issues ration card to the applicants on furnishing the
following proof/documents:

¢ Income of the household per annum,
¢ Residential status of the applicant and
© Photo identity card of the applicant issued by a Government agency.

(a) Irregularities in preserving documents- Income of the household per
annum

The categorization of the applicants under various categories of beneficiaries
is done on the basis of income criteria. It was, however, seen in audit that the
selected Circle offices falling under the jurisdiction of the New Delhi and
North West zonal offices, failed to furnish to Audit, income documents in
support of issuance of ration cards, in respect of the selected FPS and KODs,
for even a single beneficiary, to whom SFAs and kerosene oil/gas subsidy was
being provided.

Thus, the Department did not evolve a process to review and eliminate bogus
ration cards as instructed by the Gol vide its various control orders issued
from time to time.

In its reply (March 2013), the Department stated that as per retention
schedule, period of retention was two years. Therefore, the documents were
not preserved thereafter. The Department further informed that scanning of
document was now being done for all new cards. Reply of the Department
was not tenable as it did not furnish documents even in respect of APL
(Unstamped) category of cards which were issued by it during the period of
audit. Further, retention schedule as referred to in the reply was also not made
available to Audit. The destruction of documents before performing statutory
obligations of the PDS (Control) Order, 2001, regarding review of cards every
year to eliminate bogus cards and ineligible beneficiaries, was irregular.

(b) Non-credibility of beneficiaries under the LPG consuming category

A subsidy at the rate of $40 per month was approved by the Cabinet (June
2011) to those BPL and AAY cardholders who fall under the category of gas
users and do not claim kerosene oil at subsidized rate. Scrutiny of information
furnished to audit revealed that a maximum of 6748 of BPL and AAY
beneficiaries per quarter were given gas subsidy. The Department did not
provide the details of total card holders who opted for subsidy and those who
could not be provided the subsidy.

Test check of the information in respect of the selected Zonal office revealed
that 649 BPL/AAY beneficiaries under the jurisdiction of the North West
Zonal office were given gas subsidy. It was, however, noticed that the forms
for the subsidy were dispatched by post to 7312 beneficiaries, of which 2483
forms were received back undelivered and in respect of 4180 beneficiaries the
Department had neither received back the forms undelivered nor received the
filled up forms. Likewise, under the New Delhi Zonal office, gas subsidy was

102


Chapter-4 : CCO Based Audit

extended to only 188 beneficiaries. Neither the Department, nor the concerned
Zonal office/Circle offices had taken any initiative to ensure the reliability and
authenticity of beneficiaries who had not responded. In such a situation,
existence of bogus beneficiaries who are in receipt of SFAs, could not be
ruled out.

In its reply (March 2013), the Department stated that intimations were sent via
speed post to all BPL/AAY gas users and subsidy under the optional scheme
disbursed to those beneficiaries who had submitted their details. It was
presumed that being a meager subsidy, it did not evince suitable interest in the
scheme. For checking bogus cards, precaution would be taken through the
new computerized system where ration cards would be linked to Aadhar
cards.

Reply of the Department was not convincing as it did not take any initiative to
verify the actual existence of beneficiaries whose applications were received
back undelivered or who did not respond and were also in receipt of other
SFAs under PDS.

The categorization of beneficiaries determines the quantum of disbursement
of SFAs and kerosene oil/gas subsidy. Scrutiny of the records revealed that
uregular categorization led to disbursement of SFAs at higher issue prices to
the beneficiaries of Homeless and JRC category as elaborated below:

(a) In pursuance of the directions of the Supreme Court to cover homeless
population by AAY cards in six months, the GNCTD decided (July 2007) that
homeless families would be identified among the poorest of the poor. In
compliance, the Department undertook, with the help of civil society
organizations, a massive survey of homeless families in the city. In terms of
the instructions issued (August 2008), the holder of homeless ration card shall
be entitled to 15 kgs of SFAs and 10 litres of kerosene oil. However, it was
observed that the homeless families were provided with BPL cards, not
because of their socio-economic status but because the Department had
exhausted the limit (1.57 lakh) for issuing AAY cards prescribed by the
Government of India.

As per January 2012 allocation, there were 4388 homeless cards issued under
the jurisdiction of five Zonal offices of Delhi. Irregular categorization of
homeless cards led to disbursement of SFAs at higher rates to the poor and
needy people, since BPL rates were being charged instead of the highly
subsidized AAY rates.

In its reply (March 2013), the Department stated that the suggestion of Audit

to consider homeless for distribution of SFAs at AAY rates would be put up
for approval of competent authority after detailed examination

(5) As per instructions issued (March 2009) by the Department with
regard to issuing of new BPL ration cards, it was clarified that Jhuggi
dwellers, possessing valid current APL ration cards, only may apply and
convert it to new BPL cards. On acceptance of their applications for new
BPL cards, the Jhuggi APL ration card was to be surrendered and cancelled

103


Audit Report No. 3 of the year 2013

before the new BPL card was issued. It was, however, noticed that the
Jhuggi ration cards were still being categorized under the APL category and
distribution of SFAs being charged at APL rates, which were considerably
higher than the BPL rates. As per allocation for the month of March 2012,
there were 261396 beneficiaries under the Jhuggi Ration Card category. In
its reply (July 2012) the Department stated that no such category of ration
card, ie. BPL-JRC was in existence and that no category was defined as
BPL-JRC, The Department further stated (March 2013), that during the
drive, the Jhuggi dwellers who opted for new BPL cards, were given fresh
BPL cards based on the income criteria. Reply was not tenable in terms of
Department’s own instructions (March 2009), whereby JRC beneficiaries
were to be issued BPL cards. Moreover, compliance to order issued in
March 2009, was not furnished in support of contention made by the
Department.

4.2.9.5 Fair Price Shops and Kerosene Oil Depots

(a) Allotment of licences

In terms of the Department’s OM dated 18 June 1993, all Circle FSOs were to
draw a 40-point roster in respect of their circles for following reservation in
grant of FPS authorization and KOD license, as given in the Table 4.6 below:

Table 4.6; 40-point roster for FPS and KOD authorisation

rs

Scheduled Castes/ Scheduled Tribe 25%
2. Women, whose spouse is not gainfully 10% | 10%
employed
3 Ex-Servicemen/War widow 10% | 10%

Other parameters being equal, preference was to be given to the following
categories:

(a) Young widows and war widows:

(b) Physically handicapped;

(c) Cooperative Societies; and

(e) Unemployed graduates.

It was noticed that no roster at any level of hierarchy was maintained in the
Department, in the absence of which actual representation of various
categories in running FPSs/ KODs could not be ascertained. During 2011-12,
three licenses for FPSs and one KOD were allotted under PDS. No
information was made available regarding the category to which the FPSs
were allotted, whereas the KOD was stated to have been allotted to the general
category. One Circle office falling under the New Delhi Zone, admitted non-
following of the prescribed roster for granting FPS authorization and KOD
licence and assured to follow the same in future. Thus, fair implementation of
the policy for allotment of FPS and KODs could not be ascertained by Audit.

104


Chapter-4 : CCO Based Audit

The Department, in its reply (March 2013), informed that the 40 point roster
was maintained at headquarters in distribution branch and fuel branch for FPS
and KOD respectively and the category was assigned as per roster to the new
vacancy of PDS outlet anywhere in the NCT of Delhi. Reply of the
Department was not acceptable as incomplete extracts of rosters made
available for audit scrutiny revealed that the rosters were being maintained in
the form of running account and not Circle wise as stipulated in the orders
issued by the Department itself. As the representation of various categories
had not been worked out, irregular allotment of licences could not be ruled
out.

(b) Renewal of licence

In terms of clause 4 under para 3(c) of the Delhi Kerosene Oil (Export &
Price) Control Order, 1962, and Gazette order dated 30 January 2006 in
respect of FPS, issued by the Department, every licence/authorisation granted
shall be valid for a period of three years from the date of issue

Renewal of 174 out of 176 FPS/KOD licences was reported by the North
West Zonal office. It was noticed thatnine licences were renewed with a delay
ranging from 22 to 74 days. However, New Delhi Zonal office did not furnish
information and record pertaining to the renewal of licences during the year,
except for 12 cases relating to one Circle office.

Absence of proper maintenance of data bank with regard to the due date of
renewal of licences had the potential of irregular supply of SFAs and kerosene
oil to the invalid FPS and KODs.

In its reply (March 2013), the Department stated that as per extant guidelines
and circulars, renewal of licence was presently done by maintaining the
register in AC offices. The Department further assured that work relating to
the renewal of licenses would be computerized to generate an alert at least two
months in advance in respect of each PDS outlet renewal. Reply of the
Department was not tenable as the overall monitoring in respect of validity of
PDS outlets was not done by the Department. Moreover, no record was
furnished by the Department in respect of New Delhi Zonal office relating to
tenewal of licenses and no reasons were furnished for delay in renewal of
licenses in respect of North West Zonal office.

(c) Non-updation of data relating to number of functional FPSs and
KODs

Audit analysis of data of allocation of wheat and rice to FPSs for March 2012
revealed that an intimation for closure of seven FPSs being run by DSCSC
was communicated (August 2009) to the concerned Zonal offices by the
Department after a delay of more than a year (September 2010). Further, it
was noticed that out of these, five FPSs were still included for allocation for
March 2012 and that out of these in respect of two FPS, the Department was
still showing allocation for beneficiaries of APL (S) category. Likewise, one
FPS and two KODs were included in the data of March 2012 allocation,
though they had resigned from the business during June and August 2011.
The Department in its reply (March 2013), stated that during the first quarter
of 2012, the location of food grain was being switched over to new online e-
PDS system from the earlier manual system and as such few shops might have

105


Audit Report No. 3 of the year 2013

not been deleted from online system even after closure. The Department
further stated that now necessary rectification had been made. Reply of the
Department was not tenable as it did not take prompt action by intimating
closure of PDS outlets to the concerned zonal officers reflecting inadequate
monitoring. Moreover, compliance in respect of the contention made by the
Department to have undertaken necessary rectification, was not shown to
audit.

(d) Irregularities in renewal of FPS and KODs as per norms

Norms for setting up of FPSs and KODs were circulated by the Department in
August 1997 and January 2000 respectively, wherein, a new FPS could be
notified for a minimum of 1000 cards and a new KOD for a minimum of 850
cards. It was noticed that the licences of the FPS and KODs were renewed
even though the number of beneficiaries attached with concerned FPS and
KODs were far below the specified laid down limits. The deficit in terms of
the number of beneficiaries ranged from 134 to 844 (Annexure-4.10).

Ignoring the laid down norms while renewing the licences of the FPS and
KODs, again points to ineffective monitoring on the part of the Department.

In its reply (March 2013), the Department stated that deficit was noticeable
only in respect of KODs as the APL(S) cards were not allowed to draw
kerosene oil since June 2011. The Department, however, assured to take up
the matter with the GNCTD for rationalization of FPS and KODs. Reply of
the Department was not convincing as deficit was clearly visible in case of
both categories of PDS outlets and the number of beneficiaries attached with
the respective PDS outlets were inclusive of APL (S) cards.

Before commencement of a financial year, the Department projects demand
for SFAs viz. Wheat and Rice to be disbursed to beneficiaries under various
categories and sends requisition to the Gol for allocation of the same. As per
the policy, allocation of food-grains for AAY and BPL categories are made at
the rate of 35 kg per family per month. However, allocation for APL category
is made depending upon the availability of stocks allocated by Gol. The
Department furnished details (February 2011) to the Gol regarding the
number of cards under various categories to which it intended to distribute
SFAs with the request to allocate the full quota of SFAs. It was, however,
noticed that the projections made by the Department were unrealistic and not
based on the factual number of beneficiaries under various categories
(Annexure-4.11). The actual number of cards in the category of APL, BPL
and AAY were in deficit to the extent of 1386013, 35202 and 51537
respectively. Incorrect furnishing of information to the Gol led to excess
allocation in respect of AAY beneficiaries and consequently non-lifting of
wheat and rice adequate to cater to additional 51502 AAY beneficiaries and
short allocation for 1291 BPL beneficiaries for wheat and excess allocation of
23489 BPL beneficiaries for rice. Likewise, there was short allocation for
331479 and 614039 number of APL beneficiaries for wheat and rice

106


Chapter-4 : CCO Based Audit

respectively. No initiative on part of the Department was found on record to
ascertain actual requirement of SFAs and rectify the deficiency in the system.

Moreover, no projections were furnished by the Department to the Gol in
respect of Sugar and Kerosene oil for distribution to the beneficiaries of Delhi
even though the data relating to the beneficiaries eligible for the same was
available with it.

In its reply (March 2013), the Department stated that the figure sent to Gol
included APL (unstamped) cards which was almost equal to the difference as
pointed by Audit. The Department, however, clarified that it had excluded
APL (Unstamped) cards from the projection sent to Gol for the current year.
Reply of the Department was not acceptable as inclusion of APL (Unstamped)
category of cards to whom the Department was not disbursing SFAs and
kerosene oil was not justifiable. Moreover, no reply in respect of incorrect
projection of demand for BPL and AAY category of cardholders had been
furnished by the Department.

The Department also failed to furnish consolidated category and commodity-

allocation. Moreover, the actual distribution had to be rounded off, resulting in
slight variation. It was also informed that wheat and rice are not stored by the
Food and Supply Department in Delhi.

SFAs and Kerosene oil against the allocation made by the Gol and not
regarding short lifting. Non-storage of wheat and rice by the Department had.
no bearing on short allocation made by it.

(b) Allocation and lifting of food grains under Annapurna Scheme

Destitute persons of more than 65 years of age having no regular source of
income and not availing benefit of National Old Age Pension or State Pension
Scheme are entitled for 10 Kg of food grains free of cost under the scheme.
As per information furnished by the Department, there were only 11
beneficiaries under the scheme. A total of 110 kgs of wheat per month was
allocated for the beneficiaries from April 2011 to June 2011, however, no
information regarding lifting and distribution of food grains was furnished by
the Department. Scrutiny of the records relating to lifting status of food grains
as submitted by the DSCSC revealed nil allocation and lifting of food grains
under the scheme. This indicates inaction on the part of the Department to
identify beneficiaries and correlate data with other schemes. Similar
observation was also made in the C & AG’s Report for the year ended March
2011 (Para 2.1.8.4), however, instead of taking corrective action, the situation
has further deteriorated. As per information furnished by the Department, the
number of beneficiaries reduced from 128 in 2006-07 to 11 in 2011-12 and
even these beneficiaries were not forthcoming to take the quantity allocated
and hence the number of beneficiaries under the scheme was effectively nil.

The Department, in its reply (March 2013), informed that the poor response
under the scheme was due to the eligibility conditions under the scheme and
availing of benefits by the beneficiaries under other more lucrative schemes.
It was also intimated that the matter had been taken up with the GNCTD to
close the scheme, however, it was not agreed to by the GNCTD stating that
efforts be made by the Department to identify beneficiaries under the scheme.
Reply of the Department was not acceptable as efforts were lacking on the
part of the Department to identify fresh beneficiaries by way of organizing
consumer awareness programme. Moreover, the funds amounting to हैं 53.87
lakh allocated during 2000-01 remained unutilized as of March, 2013.

Chapter-4 : CCO Based Audit

(c) Irregularities in functioning of PDS outlets with regard to distribution
of SFAs and Kerosene oil

(i) Suspected fraud and diversion in distribution of SFAs

An attempt was made in audit to reconcile the distribution of SFAs and
Kerosene Oil with the quantity allocated by the Department to the concerned
PDS outlets by inspecting the sale and stock registers of the selected FPSs and
KODs. In case of one FPS, two copies of the sale register were furnished,
while on one copy, signatures of the beneficiaries were missing, whereas in
the second copy, for the same period, signatures of beneficiaries, as a token of
receiving ration, were available. Likewise, in one FPS and three KODs, the
signatures/thumb impressions in token of receiving ration, appeared to be
marked by one single individual. In other three FPSs and one KOD, absence
of signatures/thumb impressions of the beneficiaries, in token of receipt of
SFAs and kerosene oil, was also noticed.

In its reply (March 2013), the Department informed that preliminary enquiry
had been conducted by the Zonal office and show cause notice for suspension
was being issued and assured for detailed action taken report.

(ti) Insufficient disbursements to the Homeless Card Holders

The Department did not visualize and match supply with actual demand in
case of Homeless card holders. The proposal initiated by the Department and
approved by the Chief Minister of Delhi regarding disbursement of SFAs to
the families of Homeless people was to restrict the quantum of SFAs to 15
kgs, 1.९. 10 kgs of Wheat and 5 kgs of Rice or vice-versa as per their eating
habits in respect of families having only one member. Families having more
than one member were to be disbursed with 35 kgs of SFAs. Audit observed
that the Department was, however, disbursing only 15 kgs of SFAs, even to
families with more than one member.

In its reply (March 2013), the Department stated that no such data was
available with it that homeless persons were having more than one family
member and each homeless card holder was considered as one unit. Reply of
the Department was not correct as the Department itself initiated the proposal
to the Chief Minister of Delhi for restriction of SFAs to 15 kgs for homeless
persons having only one member in his family. Moreover, the Department did
not respond to the clarification sought on the same issue by the NGO-Samaya
in this regard which was involved in the survey of identification of homeless
beneficiaries.

(iii) Non-allocation of additional adhoc quota of food grains to 447
category

Scrutiny of the distribution of SFAs revealed that most vulnerable section of
society 1.९. AAY card holders had been neglected by the Department as far as
distribution of additional adhoc quota of food grains, in addition to regular
supply/quota at subsidized rates during the year 2011-12. No proposal was
initiated by the Department to address the needs of the AAY even though
adequate quantity of unlifted (14762.20 tons of wheat and 5921.20 tons of
tice) food grains was available within the regular allocated quota.

109


Audit Report No. 3 of the year 2013

In its reply, the Department stated (August 2012) that no additional/adhoc
allocation was received from the Gol for AAY category ration cards. It further
stated (March 2013) that the maximum limit of 35 kgs SFA to AAY category

35 kgs were disbursed by the Department to BPL beneficiaries.

transportation cost is passed on to the RCHs according to their categories,
except AAY category in case of which, charges are paid by the Department to
DSCSC.

4.2.12 Viability of running of FPS and KODs

Success of the Public Distribution System depends upon the survival of the
FPSs and KODs, which in turn depends upon the financial returns available to
the FPS/KOD owner. From a financial viewpoint, there must be a fair return
on the efforts and investment of owner. Viability of a FPS/KOD is multi-
dimensional phenomenon and is related to a large number of factors, such as,
number of consumers attached with the FPS/KOD, regular flow of PDS
supplies, margins permitted on gross turnover, costs of running a FPS and
interventions regarding the costs of transportation and interest charges on the
amount of funds needed for stocking. Audit scrutiny of the system revealed
poor viability conditions for operation and survival of the FPS/KOD holders
as elaborated in the subsequent paragraphs:

istence of FPS/KODs in excess of norms

It was noticed that there were 2498 and 2361 FPSs and KODs catering to
1779476 cardholders (as per allocation for March 2012). Keeping in view the
total number of cards, it was evident that FPSs were functioning with an
average of 712 beneficiaries per FPS whereas KODs were functioning at an
average of only 151 beneficiaries per KOD. Thus, there were 719 FPS and
1942 KODs in excess as per the stipulated norms. The trend of deficit number

THT (4400988 #25) + (871489 #15) = 110024700+1 3072335 = Rs. 12,30,97,035

WW


Audit Report No. 3 of the year 2013

of beneficiaries attached with the FPS and KODs was clearly observed in both
the selected Zonal offices.

Under the jurisdiction of New Delhi Zonal office, there were 157 and 224
FPSs and KODs catering to 101508 and 18066 cardholders respectively,
functioning with an average of 647 beneficiaries per FPS and 81 beneficiaries
per KOD. Likewise, in case of North West Zonal office, there were 325 and
298 FPSs and KODs catering to 276077 and 63027 cardholders respectively,
with an average of 849 beneficiaries per FPS and 212 beneficiaries per KOD.

Running of FPSs/KODs in excess of the laid down norms amounts to not only
hindering the process of keeping a check on the functioning of too many
FPS/KODs by conducting regular inspections (with deficient manpower as
brought out in para 4.2.1), but also providing poor margins to the concerned
FPS/KODs. This may encourage them to indulge in malpractices like black-
marketing, pilferage and/or diversion of SFAs and kerosene oil as commented
upon in para 4.2.10.2(c)(i), to overcome the pecuniary loss.

In its reply (March 2013), the Department stated that the deficit in the number
of beneficiaries attached with PDS outlets was due to demographic difference.
Further, PDS outlets were also being operated keeping in view the fact that the
beneficiaries would be benefitted in terms of the distances from their
residences and that closure of EPS, would lead to public resentment. Reply of
the Department was not acceptable as the criteria for minimum number of
beneficiaries to be attached with PDS outlets take care of the demographic
differences and a larger population of beneficiaries would result in opening of
more number of PDS outlets. Further, clause 5 (3) (a) of the Delhi Specified
Articles (Regulation of Distribution) Order, 1981 provides for cancellation of
authorization of FPS, in case the number of FPS in a particular area is more
than the number of FPS required to ensure proper distribution of SFAs.

of selected Circles were earning less than the minimum wages. Scrutiny of
the records relating to the selected FPSs and KODs revealed that one FPS and
three KODs resigned from the business due to poor margins earned on sales,
owing to insufficient number of beneficiaries attached with their outlets.

.2.12.3 Non-implementation of sale of non-!

In a move to encourage FPS owners to keep their FPS open throughout
official opening hours, it was decided (May 2011) to allow the sale of non-
PDS items through FPSs. The decision was taken on the basis of surveys
conducted by the Department which reported closure of many FPSs. A similar
initiative was taken earlier by the Department in May 2005 to improve the
viability of Fair Price Shops (FPS), instructing all the Assistant
Commissioners (licensing authority of PDS outlets) to allow sale of certain
non-PDS items through FPS. The decision was to be communicated to all the
FPS holders and compliance report was to be submitted in this regard. No
action was found on record to have been taken by the Department since its
meeting held in May 2011 for improving the viability of the FPS. While
Headquarters office did not respond to the audit observation, the New Delhi
Zonal office admitted (July 2012) non-availability of compliance
reports/inspection report with regard to the implementation of the decision.
Similarly, North West Zonal office confirmed (October 2012) about non-
selling of non-PDS items by the concerned FPS under its jurisdiction. In its
reply (March 2013), the Department assured that efforts would be made to
sensitize the FPS owners for availing the benefits of sale of other non-PDS
items to make it viable in terms of orders issued in 2005. The Department
further informed that the order of May 2011 was not implemented as it was
withdrawn.

of retailer margin and undue burden of

on the beneficiari

As per Memorandum of As: ion, DSCSC is authorized to lift centrally
allocated sugar from mills and supply it to various FPSs in Delhi as per
allocation made by the Department. The FPS holder in turn, is required to pay
to DSCSC the central issue price for the monthly allocation in advance.
Scrutiny of the record revealed that DSCSC paid ex-factory price to the mill
owner and claimed the difference between the ex-factory price and central
issue price with wholesale margin and transportation charges from FCI. As
per the guidelines issued by Ministry of Food, in November, 1996 the
wholesale margin and retail margin could be claimed according to the said
guidelines by Wholesaler and Retailer. The DSCSC had been regularly
claiming its margin money, transport cost and difference in price since 1996
after obtaining the approval from FCI. The wholesale margin and transport
charges claimed by the DSCSC for the year 2011-12 were % 50.32 (₹ 50.22 for
April 2011) and % 33.52 per quintal respectively. On the basis of said
guidelines the retailer margin for the year 2011-12 works out to % 22.77.
Besides, this cost of हैं 10.32 per quintal upto October 2011, which was
subsequently revised to हैं 25.32 per quintal from November 2011, was
recoverable from FPS holder on account of transportation charges payable to
DSCSC. The actual margin provided by GNCTD to FPS is ₹ 10.08 per
quintal which is not as per the said guidelines. The GNCTD had refused for
the revision of retailers margins from 1995-96 in the Margin Committee
meeting held on 3 August 2001 and the same was yet to be revised.
Moreover, the transportation cost of retailer has to be paid by the GNCTD but
the same has been recovered from economically weaker BPL cardholders.

In its reply (March 2013), the Department stated that the matter would be
reviewed.

eimbursement of margin y payable to FPS holders

The Department is responsible for reimbursing the amount of margin money
payable to FPS holders on account of sale of SFAs under Antyodaya Anna
Yojana at the rate of 35 per quintal. It was noticed that the Department did
not reimburse the margin money to the FPS holders in 2011-12. A sanction of
र₹ 1.29 crore was issued (14 March, 2012) to make payment of margin money
to FPS holders; however, the same was withdrawn by the Department (28
March 2012). The Department did not furnish year and zone-wise details of the
total amount of margin money payable to FPS holders.

In its reply (March 2013), the Department agreed that its efforts were not
adequate in the past as it required collection of data from 2500 FPSs. They
also informed that reimbursement of margin money and transportation charges
was now being taken on priority basis and the matter was under submission
for approval.

13.1 Non-implementation of revised poli n distribution of wheat

flour

In a move to curb diversion/leakages of food grains under TPDS, a revised
policy on distribution of wheat flour to ration card holders was issued
(January 2008) by the Ministry of Consumer Affairs, Food and Public
Distribution, Gol. As per the revised policy, in the beginning of every
financial year, State/UT Governments would assess the requirement of wheat
flour in their State/UT based on options, if any, to be exercised by the Ration
Card holders, and distribution of wheat flour in quantities equal to about half
of the monthly allocations of wheat to ration cardholders was to be done from
March 2008 onwards. Expenses on milling/grinding of whole wheat,
packaging and transportation of whole wheat to mills and of wheat flour from
the mills to distribution centers, etc. were to be borne by the State/UT
Governments or to be adjusted suitably in the quantity/issue price of wheat
flour in such a manner as no additional burden is passed over to the targeted
AAY, BPL and APL ration cardholder families.

Gol allocated (February 2011) monthly quantities of 12267 tons of rice and
37112 tons of wheat for AAY, BPL and APL families under the TPDS for the
period from April 2011 to March 2012, The rice allocated was to be
distributed in the form of whole grains, whereas, the wheat allocated could be
distributed as wheat flour/atta or fortified wheat flour/atta through the network
of fair price shops. Scrutiny of record and information furnished to audit
revealed that the Department had not adhered to the directions of the Ministry
and had taken no initiative with regard to obtaining of options from the ration
card holders in this regard. Instead, it distributed the entire allocated wheat in
the form of whole grains only. Non-adherence to the directives not only led to
non-extending of relief to the beneficiaries with regard to grinding charges,
but also reflected that the Department did not act to curb and curtail
diversion/leakages of food grains under the TPDS.

115


Audit Report No. 3 of the year 2013

In its reply (March 2013), the Department stated that the scheme of wheat
flour was optional and the GNCTD could not start distributing flour under
TPDS. The Department further stated that Bhagidari Atta scheme was
launched to provide fortified atta at cheaper rates than market but not under
PDS. Reply of the Department was not acceptable as the revised policy on
distribution of wheat flour was to be adopted w.e.f. March 2008 to comply
with the directions of the Supreme Court. Allocation letter issued by the Gol
(February 2011), specifically provided for distribution of wheat in the form of
flour/fortified flour. Further, the Bhagidari Atta Scheme launched by the
GNCTD was part of Open Market Sale Scheme (Domestic) and could not be
taken as a substitute for implementation of the revised policy on distribution of
wheat flour under PDS.

Tn terms of paragraph 6 (2) of Annexe to the Public Distribution System
(PDS) Control Order, 2001, State Governments shall ensure regular
inspections of FPSs not less than once in six months by the designated
authority. State Governments may issue orders specifying the inspection
schedule, list of checkpoints and the authority responsible for ensuring
compliance with the said orders. There were 2498 FPSs as per the allocation
for March 2012. As such, at least 4996 regular inspections were to be carried
out during the year. It was, however, noticed that the Enforcement Branch of
the Department carried out only 95 inspections in the year 2011-12.

Further, targets for monthly random inspection of PDS outlets were fixed for
Inspectors, FSOs and Assistant Commissioners at fifteen, ten and seven
respectively. It was observed that there were 157 FPS (as per allocation for
March 2012) functioning under the jurisdiction of the New Delhi Zonal office,
for which a minimum of 314 inspections were to be carried out. However,
only 22 surprise inspections were conducted by the vigilance and enforcement
branch during the year. Likewise, there were 325 FPSs (as per allocation for
March 2012) functioning under the jurisdiction of North West Zonal office,
entailing a minimum of 650 inspections. Against this, only 396 inspections
were conducted. Moreover, the Department failed to produce even a single
inspection report in support of the inspections claimed to have been conducted
by it. Similar observation was also made in the Report of C & AG (Civil and
Commercial) for the year ended March 2011 (Para 2.1.11.1) with the
recommendation for conducting of inspections prescribed under control orders
at each level. Non-conducting of monthly inspections as per required
frequency, showed inaction on the part of the Department to monitor the
working of FPS.

In its reply (March 2013), the Department accepted the audit contention and
stated that effective monitoring would be ensured by re-circulating the
instructions.

its redressal mechanism

No consolidated data was available with the Department to assess the number
of complaints received at each level of hierarchy. As per the information
made available to audit, 103 complaints were received at the Head office of
the department during the year 2011-12, out of which 32 were pending.
Likewise, 175 complaints (pertaining to five Circles) were received in respect
of FPSs/KODs, functioning under the overall jurisdiction of the New Delhi
Zonal office. Zonal office stated that the complaints had been forwarded to
the concerned FSO for action against the defaulters. However, it was
observed that the Zonal office was neither aware of the status of the
settlement/pendency of the complaints nor could provide the related records in
this regard, showing lack of control over the Circle offices under its
jurisdiction.

No consolidated data regarding the status of the complaints was available with
the North West Zonal office. The data compiled from the information
received from the Circle offices revealed that there were 259 complaints
(including opening balance of 81 complaint cases) in respect of seven Circle
offices, out of which 75 complaints were pending at the end of the year.
Neither any reason for the pendency nor any action report was made available
to audit. Scrutiny of the selected FPS (No. 3533) revealed that an order to
suspend the authorization of licence with immediate effect was passed on 19
August 2011 on the ground of non-distribution of SFAs (for two months) and
non-maintenance of proper accounts. ‘The case was sent back (May 2012) to
the concerned competent authority to pass orders, status of which was not
available. Department failed to furnish the status of the undisbursed quantity
of SFAs in this case as well as norms with regard to time frame for disposing
the cases.

The Department, in its reply (March 2013), stated that the complaint redressal
mechanism existed at HQ level for speedy redressal. Reply of the Department
was not convincing as no record relating to final disposal of complaint cases
was furnished to Audit at any level of hierarchy including the head office of
the Department.

In terms of the circular issued in July 2003, departmental action in case of
minor variation of stock of all the commodities mainly wheat, rice and sugar
upto 50 kg, suspension in case of variation of more than 50 kg and suspension
along-with lodging of FIR in case of variations above 100 kg is to be taken.
Similar action is to be taken in respect of the quantity variation of kerosene
oil. Scrutiny of the sale registers of the test checked FPSs and KODs revealed
that in respect of eight FPSs, there was average monthly shortage of SFAs
ranging from 57 kgs to 347 kgs, whereas, in respect of three KODs, there was
an average monthly shortage ranging from 60 to 100 litres. Apart from these,
shortages less than the above prescribed limits, were also observed in the
other selected outlets. It was, however, observed that no action had been taken

117


Audit Report No. 3 of the year 2013

at any level of hierarchy in the Department and that the shortages were being
allowed as a matter of routine, without fixing any accountability and imposing
any penalty as prescribed

In its reply (March 2013), the Department stated that detailed comments from
respective zonal office were awaited.

Delay in opening of sale ranging from four to 13 days after receipt of SFAs
was observed in the case of three selected FPS outlets falling under the
jurisdiction of New Delhi Zonal office reflecting poor monitoring by the
Zonal office and the Department and thereby giving opportunity to the FPS
holders to distort the quality and quantity of SFAs to their advantage.

The Department, in its reply (March 2013), stated that delay in opening of
sales, was on account of disbursement of SFAs at one time in order to
facilitate beneficiaries to purchase it in one visit as delivery of SFAs and sugar
at FPS outlets was not received together in some instances. It was further
informed that SFAs were normally supplied from the godown to the PDS
outlets within four hours. Reply of the Department was not acceptable in
terms of clause 6(2) of the PDS (Control) Order, 2001, which provides that
the FPS owners should take delivery of stocks from authorized nominees of
the State Government to ensure that essential commodities were available at
the FPS within first week of the month for which the allotment is made.

2 pen market operations

4.2.15.1 Non-maintenance of database of existing number of wholesalers,

millers and retailers to operationalize its mechanism of open
market sale scheme.

The Department is responsible for managing the trade and commerce in
essential commodities with a view to maintain or increase supplies there ofand
secure their equitable distribution and availability at fair prices by enforcing
the Essential Commodities Act, 1955 and various Control Orders made
thereunder, thereby fixing of stock limits for various categories of dealers
such as millers/producers, wholesalers and retailers, It was, however,
observed that the Department was having no database with regard to the
existing wholesalers, millers and retailers operating in the NCTD.

In its reply (March 2013), the Department stated that the database of
wholesalers/millers was not being maintained as the same was not required.
Reply of the Department was not tenable in terms of mandate assigned to it
which provided for fixing of stock limits, for various categories of dealers to
ensure supply of essential commodities at fair prices. In the absence of the
database, the Department was not in a position to check potential black-
marketing practices and stabilize prices of essential commodities, when
required.

in the activities under open market opera

The GNCTD in partnership with Delhi Roller Flour Mills Association
(Association), Kendriya Bhandar, NCCF and FCI decided to distribute whole
wheat Atta at reasonable rate to the general public in Delhi in order to check
the inflationary trend under the Open Market Sales Scheme of the Gol,
Ministry of Food and Public Distribution. Memorandum of Understanding
(January 2010) was signed between GNCTD through the Department of Food,
Supplies and Consumer Affairs and Delhi Roller Flour Mills Association. As
per clause 12 of the MoU, the Association would engage trucks, salesmen at
their own cost at different places, decided by the Department and make sale of
10 kg bag at the rate of € 139 and 5 kg bag at the rate of € 70. For the smooth
and successful implementation of this initiative, instructions were also issued
(November 2009) to all Joint Commissioners of the concerned districts,
Assistant Commissioners (ACs) and FSOs. In terms of the order issued, the
availability of the Atta was to be ensured by all ACs at the designated sale
point of circle office falling under their jurisdiction. Scrutiny of record made
available for audit revealed that approximately 700-800 MT of Atta was
supplied daily under the scheme as per the status conveyed by the Department
(February 2010).

Further, Whole Meal Atta Manufacturer’s Association Delhi (Regd.) (April
2010) had shown willingness to sell fortified Atta at the rate of हैं 136 per 10 kg

119


Audit Report No. 3 of the year 2013

bag and intended to participate in Bhagidari Scheme of Atta distribution at
concessional rate i.e. at no profit no loss basis by reducing milling charges and
distribution charges. Similar type of proposal was also received from Delhi
Roller Flour Mills Association (April 2010), wherein they had offered to
reduce price by ₹ 2 per 10 kg bag ie. at the rate of 137 per 10 kg bag. No
decision was taken on these proposals by the department till August, 2011.

The Department failed to furnish information regarding the monthly average
consumption of Bhagidari Fortified Atta during the year 2009-11 along with
reasons for discontinuation and status of the scheme. Similarly, non-
maintenance and availability of information with regard to the coordination of
the scheme was observed in the test check of the records at each level of
selected hierarchy.

The Department in its reply (March 2013), stated that it was not true that the
Department failed to take action and that although the file remained
unattended in the Office of the Minister of Food and Supply from May 2010
to August 2011, the matter was simultaneously dealt in another file on 30 June
2011. The Department further stated that the Whole Meal Atta Manufactures
Association, Delhi failed to furnish the requisite information as asked for in
August and October 2011 and subsequently the scheme was closed.

The reply was not acceptable as it did not respond to the fact that the
Department failed to capitalize on the offer (April 2010) made by the Delhi
Roller Flour Mills Association which was already supplying Atta under the
Bhagidari scheme and offered to further reduce the existing price. The
requisite information i.e. credentials of Association, infrastructure details etc.
were called for by the Department after a lapse of more than a year.

The Department did not evolve a strategy to check the rising prices of
essential commodities and give relief to the residents of Delhi as shown below:

(a) Inaction of the Department to control the prices of essential
commodities

During the year, a provision of = 2.50 crore was kept for market intervention
to check the rising prices of Essential Commodities. However, it was
observed that the entire amount remained unutilized at the end of the year
even though prices of some of the essential commodities reflected an upward
trend showing variation ranging from 16 per cent to 142.86 per cent within
the year as shown in Table 4.9:



Chapter-4 : CCO Based Audit

Table 4.9: Retail prices of essential commodities and vegetables in Delhi

commodity

There was abrupt increase in prices ranging from 10.05 per cent to 79.3] per
cent, between two consecutive quarters, in certain commodities during the
three year period from 2009-10 to 2011-12 (Amnexure-4.12). The Department
was requested (July 2012) to furnish information regarding its policy, norms
and criteria with regard to invoking and utilization of the provision for Market
Intervention to check the rising prices of Essential Commodities.

In its reply (March 2013), the Department stated that it acted only in case of
hoarding of essential commodities and that funds allocated by the Government
were kept as contingency fund. The Department further stated that the system
of invoking the provisions of market intervention was very elaborate and thus
could not be resorted to at short intervals. Reply of the Department was not
acceptable as it did not act to utilize the contingency fund even during
prolonged periods of price rise in respect of certain essential commodities
during 2009-10 to 2011-12.

(b) Prevention of black-marketing and maintenance of supplies of
Essential Commodities

Provision of Rule 3 of the Prevention of Black-marketing and Maintenance of
Supplies of Essential Commodities (BMSEC) Act, 1980 empowers the
Department to detain persons whose activities are found to be prejudicial to
the maintenance of supplies and commodities essential to commodity.

The Department did not undertake activity at any level by way of conducting
raids and inspections to keep a check on hoarding practices as adopted by
various categories of dealers.

The Department was undertaking negligible activities on account of open
market operations. It did not address the issues relating to upward trend of
prices of essential commodities, either by way of evolving a strategic plan to
utilize the funds allocated to it or by carrying out inspections and raids to keep
a check on black marketing practices.

The Department in its reply (March 2013) assured to create database to
control black-marketing.

121


Audit Report No. 3 of the year 2013

4.2.16 Human Resource Managemen!

Optimum utilization of available resources is the key factor in deciding the
outcome of the activities undertaken by the Department, to achieve objectives
and fulfill the mandate. As of March 2012, the Department was functioning
with manpower of 624 employees facing a shortage of 518 personnel. It was,
however, observed that instead of utilizing the services of available men-in-
position appropriately, the Department was utilizing the services on unfruitful
activities in the form of deputing its staff on processing and preparation of
ration cards to be issued to APL-unstamped category. As referred to in para
4.28.1, issuing of ration cards to the aforementioned category was without any
justification since no benefit in terms of subsidy or SFAs were being
distributed to them.

The Department in its reply (March 2013), stated that the matter regarding
utilization of services on unfruitful activities like issuing of APL (unstamped)
cards would be examined. Reply was not convincing as the Department
needed to utilize its available manpower optimally to fulfill its mandate
prudently.

The CCO based audit has brought out that the internal control mechanism in
the Department of Food and Supplies, GNCTD, is deticient, leading to
financial mismanagement, weak budgetary control as well as inadequate
monitoring of the FPSs and KODs. Deficient manpower of the Department
further compounded the deficiencies. This has resulted in improper updation of
ration cards. Contravention of procedures also led to unnecessary burdening
of the beneficiaries. Approvals related to compensation to the DSCSC at the
cost of both the FPS and beneficiaries were not in order. The Department has
not considered the issue of viability of FPS/ KODs. The Department was not
carrying out all its mandated roles, especially in respect of the open market
interventions even though it was allocated funds every year. Despite abrupt
sharp increases in the prices of certain essential commodities, it did not
develop a strategy for the same. Non maintenance of essential data base
regarding the shop owners, wholesalers, millers, retailers etc, limited the
capacity of the Department for meaningful intervention in case of necessity.

Recommen

> Initiative needs to be taken to identify beneficiaries under various
categories and reviewed on regular basis to eliminate bogus cards.

»& Mechanism for updation of beneficiaries as well as FPS and KODs needs
to be strengthened as well as monitored.



Chapter-4 : CCO Based Audit

Vv

Internal control mechanism of the Department needs to be strengthened.

Vv

Office Procedure Manual based on_functional requirement needs to be
developed in order to create awareness of working procedures to be
followed at each level of hierarchy.

v

Timely steps and follow-up action need to be taken to ensure viability of
running FPSs and KODs.

v

Department should develop strategy for its role in open market
interventions and implement the same accordingly.

New Delhi (DOLLY CHAKRABARTY)
Dated: Principal Accountant General (Audit), Delhi

Countersigned

New Delhi (VINOD RAI)
Dated: Comptroller and Auditor General of India

Annexures

Annexure-4.4
(Refers to footnotes given in the text of the main report)

Rule 118 of Receipt and Payment Rules 1983 stipulates that a certificate shall be attached to
every abstract contingent bill to the effect that the detailed contingent bills have been
submitted to the controlling officer in respect of abstract contingent bills drawn during the
month previous to that in which the bill in question is presented for payment. On no account
may an abstract contingent bill be cashed without this certificate.

Rule 176 of GER states that under some special circumstances, it may become necessary to
select a particular consultant where adequate justification is available for such single- source
selection in the context of the overall interest of the Ministry of Department. Full justification
for single source selection should be recorded in the file and approval of the competent
authority obtained before resorting to such single-source selection.

Rule 184 of GER states that should it become necessary in an exceptional situation to
outsource a job to a specifically chosen contractor, the Competent Authority in the Ministry or
Department may do so it consultation with the Financial Advisor, In such cases the detailed
justification, the circumstances leading to the outsourcing by choice and the special interest or
purpose it shall serve shall form an integral part of the proposal.

CVC order No.75/12/04 dated the 24th December, 2004, which states that A firm/ consultant
which has been engaged to provide consulting services for the preparation or implementation
of a project, and any of its affiliates, will be disqualified from subsequently providing goods
or works or services related to the initial assignment for the same project.

Rule 57 of GFR 2005 states that ‘no expenditure shall be incurred during a financial year on a
‘New Service’ not contemplated in Annual Budget for the year except after obtaining
supplementary grant or appropriation or an advance from the Contingency Fund during the
year.

Rule 10 (4) of Delegation of Financial Powers Rules on ‘Appropriation and Re-appropriation-
General Restrictions’ states that funds shall not be appropriated or re-appropriated to meet
expenditure on new service or new instrument of service not contemplated in the budget as
approved by Parliament.

Rule 5(6) (0) of Transaction of Business rule, 1993 of GNCTD states that nothing in this rule
shall be construed as authorizing any authority or Department, including the Finance
Department to appropriate or re-appropriate funds to meet expenditure on a new service not
contemplated in the budget as approved by the Legislative Assembly

161


Audit Report No. 3 of the year 2013

Footnote (0)

Footnote (12)

Rule 5(2) (a) and 5 (6) (d) of Transaction of Business rule, 1993 of GNCTD states that unless
the proposal is fully covered by the power to sanction expenditure or to appropriate or re-
appropriate funds conferred by any general or special orders made by the Finance
Department, no Department shall, without the previous concurrence of the Finance
Department, issuc any order which may involve any abandonment of revenue or involve any
expenditure for which no provision has been made in the Appropriation Act and nothing in
this rule shall be construed as authorizing any authority or Department, including the Finance
Department to appropriate or re-appropriate funds to meet expenditure on a new service not
contemplated in the budget as approved by the Legislative Assembly.

Sub rule (3) of rule 116 of the Central Motor Vehicles Rules, 1989 stipulates that the
measurement for smoke emission level and Carbon Monoxide level for vehicles etc. shall be
done with a meter of the type approved by any agency referred to in rule 126 of the principal
rules or by the National Environmental Engineering Research Institute, Nagpur-440 001
provided that such a testing agency shall follow ISO or ECE Standards and procedures for
approval of measuring meters
